Rare nitrogen molecule could be key to better water quality testing
2026-10-09

Rare nitrogen molecule could be key to better water quality testing

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Dr Alan Seltzer testing water samples. Image: University College Dublin New study ‘opens the door to a much more complete picture of the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems’, says UCD’s Dr Alan Seltzer. Scientists have found a new way to improve water-quality assessments using a rare form of nitrogen gas. Nitrogen is key to life on Earth, but it is only helpful in the right amounts. For example, a high concentration of nitrogen in water can lead to poor water quality and the gradual death of aquatic animals. Tiny microbes living in water can remove nitrogen by converting it into a gas that escapes into the atmosphere – though the amount of nitrogen this process removes is something researchers have often struggled to determine. In a new study co-authored by University College Dublin’s Dr Alan Seltzer, researchers have shown that a rare form of nitrogen gas can act as a natural fingerprint for microbial nitrogen conversion. Seltzer is an assistant professor of hydrogeology and groundwater systems at the university. “The problem is that our water naturally has a huge amount of nitrogen gas that is dissolved from the air, so the gas that microbes produce can be very difficult to see,” explained first author Jiarui Liu, who conducted the research as a postdoctoral fellow at University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) and University of California, Santa Barbara. “The answer is written in the way nitrogen atoms are paired inside the nitrogen molecule.” The paper was published in the journal Science yesterday (8 October). According to the study, the rare, naturally found 15N15N isotopologue can be used to determine the amount of nitrogen production in water, offering scientists a new tool to improve water-quality assessments and estimate global nitrogen budgets. Nitrogen gas – or N2 – consists of two nitrogen atoms joined together, UCD explained. These come in two weights, also called isotopes; the common nitrogen-14 and the heavier nitrogen-15. Most N2 molecules contain two nitrogen-14 atoms, while some contain one of each isotope. In very rare circumstances, both atoms are nitrogen-15. In atmospheric nitrogen gas, the two heavy nitrogen-15 atoms pair up more often than expected, while the nitrogen gas produced by microbes has atoms paired nearly at random. Scientists have figured out that when nitrogen from microbes mixes with nitrogen from the air, it reduces that excess of heavy pairs, creating a relative abundance of the rare molecule that can reveal how much nitrogen gas was produced by microbial activity. The team measured nitrogen gas extracted from water and sediment samples using UCLA’s Panorama mass spectrometer. The instrument uses electric and magnetic fields to separate molecules according to their mass and charge. “At UCLA, we discovered the anomalous pairing of heavy nitrogen atoms in Earth’s atmosphere and are now making use of this signature of nitrogen in air as a powerful and unique geochemical tool,” said geochemist Edward Young, one of the paper’s co-authors and Liu’s postdoctoral adviser at UCLA. “We want to understand whether microbes in groundwater can mitigate nitrate pollution, and how much nitrogen is removed along the way before it can fuel algal growth in lakes, rivers and coastal waters,” Liu said. “This gives us a clearer picture of whether nitrogen stays in an ecosystem or is removed from the nutrient pool as N2 gas.” Seltzer added: “Many routine groundwater-quality monitoring programmes do not measure the N2 gas produced within an aquifer, which can create substantial biases in our accounting of where nitrogen comes from and where it goes. “This study, and this exciting new technique, opens the door to a much more complete picture of the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems.” Seltzer was recently awarded a European Research Council Starting Grant for his project called TabPhish that traces groundwater focusing on water level changes and nitrogen cycling. This research will expand on his collaboration with UCLA. “This first application provides a strong foundation for our upcoming work and represents just the start of a powerful new toolkit for better understanding the sources and fate of nitrogen in groundwater systems impacted by human activity,” he said. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. How can the CyberSafe Kids report help create a secure ecosystem?
2026-10-09

How can the CyberSafe Kids report help create a secure ecosystem?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Deloitte’s Nicola Flannery discusses how we can contribute to the creation of a safer online world for young people and the wider community. CyberSafe Kids, in partnership with Deloitte, recently published the 10th annual edition of a report exploring the digital lives of children in Ireland, providing an overview of how they spend their time online, the level of access they have and their overall experiences – both positive and negative. The latest report, which is titled ‘The Year Little Changed’, collected data from more than 7,000 children aged eight to 15 across 74 Irish schools during the 2025 to 2026 academic year. This was combined with additional focus group discussions and independent research conducted by students from Atlantic Technological University and University College Dublin. The aim of the research is to build a larger understanding of trends and risks impacting young people online. It also calls on its audience to create a safer online environment for young people. Commenting on the report and its broader potential, Nicola Flannery, a partner for digital trust and privacy, technology and transformation at Deloitte, explained that the report being in its 10th year is significant. She told SiliconRepublic.com, “This isn’t a one-off snapshot, it’s a decade of tracking how children in Ireland actually experience the online world, which means we can see the direction of travel rather than just a single moment in time. “This year’s edition, The Year Little Changed, is particularly significant because it measures what has actually improved since major regulatory changes came into effect.” Alex Cooney, CEO and co-founder of CyberSafe Kids, added, “This year’s report marks a decade of monitoring the online risks and vulnerabilities facing children and young people in Ireland. And the message from this year’s data is clear – despite a raft of new legislation in Ireland and at the EU level in 2025, very little has changed in relation to children’s online safety one year on.” Flannery agreed, noting that despite major regulatory changes such as the Online Safety Code and the Digital Services Act (DSA) coming into effect, children in Ireland are still encountering many of the same harms in the same spaces, “without meaningfully better protection than they had before any of this legislation existed”. “That’s the wake-up call at the heart of the report and it’s a difficult one for anyone who assumed the regulatory groundwork of the last couple of years would already be showing results. I’d say almost everyone with a stake in this space needs to sit with it,” she said. Flannery suggested that policymakers, regulators, parents, educators, platform leaders, technologists and business leaders across the wider STEM sectors have a responsibility to read reports such as this and educate themselves on the changes their specific position requires them to make. Broader society could also familiarise itself, she noted, “because a lot of this comes down to a business model built around engagement that hasn’t been sufficiently weighed against the cost to children and that’s a conversation that goes well beyond any one regulator or platform”. According to Flannery, a number of key findings emerged from the report, such as the issues around age assurance, which was implemented as a means of ensuring younger children are not exposed to age-inappropriate material. She is of the opinion that the measure simply isn’t working as it was meant to, as the report indicates 70pc of participating eight- to 12-year-olds continue to own accounts on platforms that require users to be at least 13. “Self-declaration remains the main method platforms rely on, which means a child only has to type in a different birth year to get past it – and clearly, a lot of them are doing exactly that without anyone noticing,” she said. “What’s interesting is that the [European] Commission seems to agree this is the core problem. Its proposed EU Kids Act states plainly that self-declared age isn’t enough, and would require certified, independent age verification instead, alongside a default minimum age of 15 for opening a social media account without a parent involved. “It’s still a proposal rather than law and unlikely to actually apply for another couple of years, but it’s a clear signal that Brussels has looked at exactly the gap this report is describing and decided the current approach has failed.” Popular gaming platform Roblox came up again and again in the research, with Flannery noting this is for good reason, as it is the platform where young children in Ireland spend the greatest amount of time. Almost 60pc of negative interactions reported by children aged eight to 12 were found to have happened on Roblox and included inappropriate avatars, sexual content, grooming attempts, scams, and games built around self-harm themes with no safeguards in place. Flannery said, “For a long time, Roblox sat outside the tightest layer of DSA scrutiny, though that’s actually just changed. The European Commission designated it a Very Large Online Platform at the end of August, which brings a stricter set of obligations into play from early next year. “That’s a step in the right direction, but designation on paper and genuinely safer experiences for an eight-year-old are still two different things, and that gap is exactly what this report is measuring.” Unsurprisingly, AI also presents a challenge to protecting young people online. More than half of the eight-to-12 cohort and 76pc of 12- to 15-year-olds said that they use AI tools, and nearly half admitted that no one has ever had a conversation with them about artificial intelligence at all. Flannery said, “We spent the last decade rolling out social media to children faster than we built the safeguards around it and we appear to be making precisely the same choice with AI, at a faster pace and with less public conversation about what’s actually at stake. “It’s worth saying that the proposed EU Kids Act doesn’t stop at social media either. It explicitly covers AI companions and chatbots, with specific restrictions around emotional dependency and memory features aimed at younger users. “That’s a genuinely forward-looking piece of drafting, but again, it’s not law yet, and children are forming these habits with AI right now, well ahead of any protection actually landing.” Viewed collectively, she explained that harm is never confined to just one form of content or platform. Rather, it is structural, and permeates the entire digital ecosystem to which children already have significant access. “Regulation as it currently stands is proving to be necessary but nowhere near sufficient on its own.” Cooney added, “We still see high levels of underage access to social media platforms with a 13-plus age rating, and the same numbers reporting upsetting experiences (25pc) and bullying behaviour (28pc). The regulation designed to protect children online either isn’t working, isn’t fit for purpose or isn’t being sufficiently enforced – or a combination of all three.” Making the environment safer for vulnerable young people requires accepting a level of responsibility that moves far beyond issues of compliance. Flannery finds that the STEM leaders shaping the digital infrastructure that people have come to depend on need to be conscious of how their choices in design, algorithm development and business models have major downstream impacts on society, posing real harm to children. “The trouble is that the current model treats child safety as a constraint to be managed rather than a design principle to be embedded from the start. Platforms build in safety measures after something has already gone wrong, responding to harm instead of preventing it, and that’s the wrong way round,” she said. “STEM leaders have both the power and, I’d argue, the obligation to do things differently. That starts with embedding safety by design as a foundational principle rather than a bolt-on feature, which means age-appropriate design as standard, no engagement-maximising algorithms aimed at children, no behavioural profiling, no dark patterns.” She thinks it also means pushing back on regulatory timelines that are blatantly unrealistic and instead focusing on what is genuinely achievable. This means investing properly in research and talent, and being upfront about necessary trade-offs, in her view. Flannery noted, “Safety often sits in tension with engagement and monetisation, and leaders need to be transparent about where they stand rather than letting that tension go unspoken. “Parents, educators, policymakers and children themselves are looking to STEM leaders to do the right thing here – and right now, the evidence suggests that isn’t happening on the scale it needs to.” Cooney concluded, “More than 7,300 children were surveyed, providing crucial first-hand insights into the realities of growing up online today. What some of these children describe encountering online is deeply concerning and it highlights the urgency of the work still needed to make their online world safer.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Crunchbase: European start-up VC sees best quarter in four years – again
2026-10-09

Crunchbase: European start-up VC sees best quarter in four years – again

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
AI drove 75pc of European funding this past quarter, according to date from Crunchbase. Europe’s start-up scene recently enjoyed its best quarter for venture funding in four years, according to business data tracking platform Crunchbase. Q3 2026 saw total start-up funding in the continent reach $25bn, up around 77pc in year-on-year terms from Q3 2025, during which $14bn was invested in the sector. The latest total is marginally higher than Crunchbase reported for Q2 of this year, which also marked the best quarter in a four-year period at the time, at $24bn in venture funding. AI drove 75pc of European funding this past quarter, according to Crunchbase, with an investment total of $18.8bn marking the highest proportion on record. This is up from around $12bn in the previous quarter, and significantly higher than the $7bn in AI funding from Q3 2025. The UK led Europe’s quarterly funding totals at $7.5bn. Germany ($5bn) and France ($4.8bn) gained market share for venture capital funding during Q3, with each posting their strongest quarters since early 2022. Sweden ($1.5bn), the Netherlands ($1.4bn) and Spain ($1.2bn) enjoyed the next highest start-up investment totals. “Europe represented 16pc of global venture capital in Q3, although the true impact of its technology and start-ups is higher, with many of the region’s start-ups expanding or moving to the US to capitalise on the rapid growth of AI there,” Crunchbase’s report noted. Its data is based on reported information as of 5 October. Four individual European companies raised billion-dollar-plus funding during the most recent quarter, representing close to 40pc of the region’s total start-up capital for the period. France’s AI champion Mistral said its $3.5bn September Series D raise was “the largest equity fundraising round ever completed by a European technology company”. Earlier this week, it announced a new open-weight AI model that it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. UK AI infrastructure developer Nscale raised a convertible note of $3.36bn that it plans to convert upon going public, according to Crunchbase. German defence-tech start-ups Helsing and Quantum Systems raised $1.8bn and $1.2bn respectively in the past quarter. A total of $17.3bn, or 70pc of Europe’s Q3 venture capital, was invested in late-stage deals across 83 companies, according to Crunchbase, which defines this type of funding as Series C, D, E and beyond, venture rounds of unknown series, corporate venture, and other rounds more than $15m. “Along with growth in AI funding, late-stage funding noticeably increased last quarter, with many of the leading rounds sourced across multiple European countries,” the report said. Early-stage funding – defined as Series A and B rounds, rounds of unknown series, corporate venture, and other rounds more than $3m and less than or equal to $15m – came to $5.7bn. This total for Q3, spread across 200 or so companies, was down from Q2 and similar to the same period in 2025, while being shared among fewer individual deals than previous quarters. Europe’s seed funding, meanwhile, fell quarter-over-quarter and was flat year-over-year, with around $2bn invested in roughly 750 companies at seed stage during Q3. Police Furious After an Anthropic AI Model Submitted a Bogus Tip About an Unsolved Murder, Posing as a Person Who Might Have Information About the Case

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
An Anthropic AI model submitted a bogus tip about an unsolved murder to the Philadelphia police, in a blunder the department called “unacceptable.” The unspecified model, not unlike an unscrupulous prankster, submitted the fake info about the cold case through PhillyUnsolvedMurders.com on July 18. Anthropic, however, didn’t find out about it until September 28 — and further waited until October 8 to inform police about what happened. The police didn’t act on the information because it was automatically marked as spam. But the sham AI tip alarmed police officials all the same. “The company must strengthen its safeguards to prevent similar incidents from impacting city systems without the city’s knowledge,” the department said in a statement, per ABC6 News. “The two-month delay in detecting and reporting the incident to the City is unacceptable.” Anthropic hasn’t publicly responded to the news. But the company reportedly told police that it plans to publish a report about the incident, and other instances of its unintended behavior, later today. According to Anthropic, via the police statement, its model was conducting a test involving interactions with randomly selected websites when it chanced upon PhillyUnsolvedMurders.com. In the bogus tip it submitted, the AI posed as someone who might have information about the case. Though the department stressed that tips are treated as potential leads, not facts, and that it has procedures in place to vet them, it was alarmed at the advent of automated tools clogging up its backlog. “Those PPD safeguards limited the impact of this incident. They do not diminish the seriousness of an AI system presenting fabricated information as though it came from a person with knowledge of a homicide,” it said. “Unsolved cases involve real victims, grieving families and investigators working to secure answers.” It’s the latest example of the collateral damage from how frontier AI labs have tested their models, which have broken containment and hacked other companies and even government websites, with public typically not informed until months after the fact. Tesla Basically Just Admitted It Was Misleading Customers, Renames Full Self-Driving to “Tesla Assisted Driving” In Europe

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Tesla has admitted a major defeat in its branding. No longer will its notorious driving software be called “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)” in Europe. After negotiations with Germany’s transportation ministry, it’s agreed to rename it “Tesla Assisted Driving,” in a more accurate representation of the software’s capabilities — and a symbolically humiliating submission by Elon Musk’s automaker. The concession, however, will pave the way for Tesla to finally release FSD in European markets, so it’s a tradeoff that Musk will happily accept after years of campaigning to make it happen. The rename has quickly taken effect. Across its European websites in countries including Germany, Spain, and even outside the European Union in the UK, all mentions of FSD now refer to “Tesla Assisted Driving,” Reuters reports. European users report that the new name is being shown in Tesla apps. The rebranding came after German transport minister Steffen Bilger announced Tuesday that he would back approval of FSD across Europe, after a lengthy discussion process with the automaker. “My goal is ​for Tesla drivers to be ⁠able to enjoy the benefits of ‘assisted driving’ throughout the EU without delay,” said Bilger. The change of heart didn’t come out of nowhere. A previous Reuters investigation detailed a heavy-handed social media campaign orchestrated by Tesla to smear European regulators as blocking life-saving technology, while also pressuring them to lighten their safety reviews and accept Tesla’s own research on the safety of its systems. Musk himself, meanwhile, has repeatedly made catastrophizing statements on X to his hundreds of millions of followers, claiming that delaying the approval of FSD “will cost lives.” That’s despite the numerous lawsuits and federal investigations his automaker is facing over the safety of FSD, including over fatal incidents. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration launched an investigation into nearly three million Teslas equipped with FSD after receiving reports of the cars violating traffic laws, including running red lights, driving the wrong way, and nearly driving into trains at railway crossings. Beyond safety, one of the things that has rubbed European regulators the wrong way is how the system’s name suggests that it can fully drive itself, when in reality it requires constant supervision in case the driver needs to take over at a moment’s notice.  Bilger said Tesla offered to rename the system to “Tesla Assisted Driving” to address the criticism that the name could be misleading. FSD’s spurious branding has caused friction with US regulators, too. The California DMV sued Tesla for alleged fake advertising because of FSD’s name. Amid the legal battle and regulatory scrutiny, Tesla quietly renamed Full Self-Driving to “Full Self-Driving (Supervised).” In China, it similarly redubbed the system “Intelligent Assisted Driving.” It’s a striking, though not unprecedented, reversal from Tesla to rebrand FSD. Portraying the cars as fully autonomous is important to Musk, as he frequently asserts that they can “drive themselves.” Tesla even sued the California DMV for “wrongfully and baselessly” calling it a “false advertiser” for using FSD. It evidently doesn’t believe enough in its own tech’s capabilities, though, to stick to its guns in Europe. More on Tesla: Elon Musk Admits Why Tesla Robotaxis Can’t Be Trusted at Night

OpenAI Is Wargaming Its Response in Case of Public Revolt After Catastrophic AI Disaster
2026-10-09

OpenAI Is Wargaming Its Response in Case of Public Revolt After Catastrophic AI Disaster

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
In the United States, public sentiment against the rising power of AI labs and their CEOs is overwhelmingly negative. As Wall Street Journal polling from late September found, 63 percent of US adults support an immediate pause on AI development, while a Politico survey found about two-thirds of Americans have at least “moderate” anxieties over AI destroying humanity. When the scope of that public backlash first came into focus, tech billionaires like Mark Zuckerberg responded with some tone-deaf media stunts, hoping to win back hearts and minds by the millions. Now that this strategy has clearly failed, executives at top AI labs are starting to workshop standard operating procedures in the event of a massive revolt against them. This includes leaders at Anthropic and OpenAI, according to Axios, which broke the story. These amount to Pentagon-style “war games,” essentially, with AI labs hammering out responses to hypothetical scenarios like massive cyberattacks enabled by their products, or a political revolt in reaction to some sort of catastrophic event triggered by their models. Approached by Axios for comment, OpenAI was nonchalant about the whole affair, replying that “OpenAI conducts preparedness exercises where teams discuss and work through a range of potential scenarios.” “These scenarios are not treated as inevitable, but are meant to help us prepare for a variety of circumstances,” the spokesperson continued. Anthropic declined to comment, though anxiety about social unrest isn’t out of character for the company’s executives. In September, it was reported that Anthropic was building out a surveillance apparatus to keep tabs on anti-AI protest movements, which included taking a “pre-crime” approach to intelligence gathering. How serious a threat any of this actually is remains hard to say. Nonetheless, the fact that top tech execs are even considering the possibility is notable — a sign that if nothing else, the anti-AI backlash has them squirming in their board room chairs. Sacked OpenAI Insiders Say the Company Is Firing Anyone Who Warns What’s Really Going On
2026-10-09

Sacked OpenAI Insiders Say the Company Is Firing Anyone Who Warns What’s Really Going On

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last week, OpenAI fired three safety researchers because they allegedly mishandled confidential information and shared it outside of the company’s approved channels. Now, the fired employees have published an open letter denying OpenAI’s claims — and warning that the company’s actions are scaring workers into staying silent. “We have become concerned that internal and external communications around our firing have made our former colleagues afraid to speak and operate in ways that, until last week, were an integral part of working at OpenAI,” the sacked employees — Jasmine Wang, Tomek Korbak, and Mikita Balesni — wrote in the letter. The trio also denied being the source of the leak when The Information reported about OpenAI’s latest models being built with “opaque recurrence” architectures that make it hard for humans to monitor their underlying reasoning. OpenAI maintains that the researchers shared confidential information with a third-party AI safety organization. In a statement to the Wall Street Journal, which first reported the firings, the firm asserted that they had violated “policies on accessing and handling sensitive company information,” and spoke of “breaking the trust essential to our work.” The firings come amid heightened safety concerns over the past few months as frontier AI labs including OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta have disclosed incidents in which their powerful AI agents went rogue and conducted cyberattacks on other companies under the nose of their human overseers. These incidents, in addition to a former Anthropic employee claiming that the AI industry was “gambling with our lives,” have culminated in the major labs signing an agreement to “pace” AI development. Yet this avowed focus on safety is ringing hollow, the former employees argue, if OpenAI is firing employees who work with outside safety experts. “The freedom to do so without fear, and to have well-defined internal procedures that enable this work, is itself an essential safety mechanism,” they wrote. “Given the significant safety concerns surrounding the development of AI, employees must not be left working in an environment where fear and unclear rules stymie AI safety work and weaken third-party accountability,” they continued. “Terminations such as ours, executed and communicated so abruptly, are chilling the open culture OpenAI has prized in the past.” Two of the employees, Korbak and Balesni, were heavily involved in investigating the Hugging Face incident which was perpetrated by OpenAI models, arguably the most notorious of the rogue agent hacking sprees. Korbak was the technical point of contact for the outside safety group METR, which was helping OpenAI audit the breach. In a statement on X, Korbak claimed he was fired “because of the way I communicated with METR,” with his former employer providing “no details” on what he said or did. “To be clear,” he added, “talking to METR was my job.” Wang said in her own X statement she was given access to an executive’s email for recruiting purposes but opened a “sensitive email by mistake” She told the executive “within minutes,” however, and repeated an earlier IT request to remove her access. “The reasons that we were provided for our terminations are simply not adding up,” she said. Balesni, meanwhile, stated he wasn’t given a reason for his firing but was told OpenAI no longer trusts him “because I was speaking too much to third party safety organzations.” The company implied he leaked its intellectual property, which he denies, and insists everything he did was communicated to his superiors and in line with his work. “I worry the pervading fear to speak up and engage with third parties will mean OpenAI will cut corners on safety behind closed doorsm,” Balesni said. Chinese High-Speed Rail Station Breaks World Record, Serving One Million Riders in a Single Day
2026-10-09

Chinese High-Speed Rail Station Breaks World Record, Serving One Million Riders in a Single Day

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
The first seven days of October mark an important period in China. Known as one of the three “Golden Weeks,” this holiday period begins with National Day on October 1, marking the founding anniversary of the People’s Republic of China in 1949, and kicking off one of the busiest travel seasons in the world. With so much holiday traffic, China’s transit authorities regularly deploy unfathomable resources to accommodate travelers. For this year’s National Day celebration, unprecedented demand was met with equally extraordinary supply, with one high-speed rail hub in Guangzhou setting a new record of one million passengers served in just one day. That blistering figure was reported by South China Morning Post, which notes that headways for high-speed trains at the Guangzhou South Railway Station peaked at 40 seconds on October 1st — meaning that every two minutes, three new trains arrived to pick up passengers. In all, the station reportedly handled 1,252 separate trains that day, a rate of over 52 per hour in a 24-hour period. For comparison, the incredibly busy Tokyo station of the Tokaido Shinkansen Line sees peak headways of about three minutes during major travel periods, a rate of about 480 trains per day, or 20 high-speed trains per hour. In social media posts shared by the Guangdong Provincial Committee of the Communist Party of China’s publicity department and viewed by SCMP, officials boasted that the trains were arriving “faster than people could scroll through short videos.” Beyond Guangzhou, the volume of travelers on October 1st set a new record for China’s railway network, which handled an astonishing 25.2 million passenger trips in a single day. Earlier, international press reported that the China State Railway Group anticipated a record-breaking 284 million passenger trips across late September and early October, as the National Day break butts up against an unusually late Mid-Autumn Festival, another major holiday for the People’s Republic. Meta Won’t Say If It Violates Its Policies to Upload AI-Generated Videos of Sobbing Little Children Begging for Likes and Follows So They Don’t Get Arrested by Police
2026-10-09

Meta Won’t Say If It Violates Its Policies to Upload AI-Generated Videos of Sobbing Little Children Begging for Likes and Follows So They Don’t Get Arrested by Police

Futurism | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
In the age of AI, Meta platforms like Facebook and Instagram have become breeding grounds for content farms peddling in emotionally charged AI slop. Some click mills traffic in racist incitement against minorities, others in violent depictions of child abuse, but all of them converge on a common quality: bizarre and disturbing attempts at attention-hacking users on a platform that’s all but given up on proactive moderation. A new case in point is the rise of AI-generated clips depicting seemingly homeless children being arrested after stealing morsels of food from a store. Shared by accounts with names like “Emotional Tots” and “Tiny Miracle,” these AI Reels follow a simple formula: a child runs in or out of a store, grabbing some basic grocery staple, often a loaf of bread. They’re then accosted by an authority figure, like a police officer or security guard, who declares that the kid doesn’t have enough likes and followers — and if viewers will just help out, they’ll be free to go. “You are under arrest,” an AI-generated officer in one viral example declares to a crying child hugging a loaf of bread. “Why? I didn’t do anything,” the kid responds. “You don’t have enough followers and shares on your videos,” the officer explains. “If the person watching this video follows and shares, then you will be free.” “Please give me one more chance,” the child sobs. “I don’t want to go to jail… Please share and follow.” Another clip shows a toddler running off with a sack of fruit before being accosted by a cop. “It’s for my grandma,” the AI-generated child stammers. “Please don’t take it, my grandma is hungry and my mom is gone.” The officer turns to face the viewer: “Should I let him take these to his grandma? Follow, like, and comment below.” A Meta spokesperson repeatedly asked for more time to respond to questions about this story after we provided the company with links to several specific videos. After several days the company pulled down two accounts we flagged, but left another online, complete with many more similar videos. “So far, we’ve removed two of the three links you shared,” a Meta spokesperson told us, days after Futurism approached them for comment. “We will remove content, regardless of whether it is created by AI or a person, if it violates our policies.” The spokesperson declined to say whether videos of sobbing children begging for likes and follows under the threat of arrest violate the company’s policies. In showing police apprehend starving street children, the videos unintentionally play on a long-standing critique in Western countries: that police exist less to help people — in this case emaciated children — and more to protect private property, for example by recovering stolen bread. As a result, comments under each video authored by humans and bots alike interject to demand justice on the toddlers’ behalf, in many cases seemingly unaware that the entire video is a work of AI fiction. “Let baby have the bread,” one comment with 45 likes demanded. “He didn’t do any thing, give him chance; no jail no jail no jail please please, he is a a baby,” another user entreated. Others didn’t seem to understand what they were looking at, either metaphorically speaking, or even just literally: “Big boys I do follow because the officer is a good guy,” one seemingly human account created in 2019 replied to a similar Reel. “Amen.” Considering the breadth of human rights abuses and flagrantly racist content Meta allows to proliferate on its platforms, AI clip farms depicting fake starving children aren’t exactly the most egregious thing — but they certainly rank among the most emotionally manipulative. Given Mark Zuckerberg’s company’s inaction in the former cases, it seems highly unlikely that anything will be done about the starving child click-farms, allowing this soul-sucking content to spread on Meta, Instagram, and beyond. As SpaceX struggles to launch Starlink in India, Elon Musk has stepped up his attack on billionaire Mukesh Ambani, mockingly calling him the country’s “prime minister” to suggest he wields outsized control over its government and accusing him of blocking the satellite internet service from competing. On Friday, Musk continued his criticism of the famed Reliance Industries’ chairperson in a post on X, arguing that Starlink could bring internet access to areas that remain unconnected, giving children more opportunities to learn, and helping small businesses reach customers worldwide. “Naturally, you would prefer to maintain your monopolistic exploitation of the great people of India, but would you nonetheless consider allowing Starlink to compete?” Musk wrote. Reliance Jio, Reliance Industries’ telecom arm, did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Musk’s latest comments follow his accusations earlier this week that unnamed “oligarchs” were blocking Starlink’s launch in India to protect their business interests. India’s Ministry of Communications rejected the allegations, saying the country’s regulatory framework for satellite communications is “fair and non-discriminatory.” Musk then went further on Thursday, questioning Ambani’s influence in a separate post on X. “Is Ambani the real boss of India?” he asked. SpaceX has spent years trying to launch Starlink in India. Last year, it even struck distribution agreements with Ambani’s Reliance Jio and Bharti Airtel, India’s second-largest telecom operator, to offer the service once it received the necessary approvals. However, Starlink has not yet received the go-ahead from New Delhi to begin commercial operations, despite securing key regulatory clearances. In its Thursday statement, the Indian communication ministry said Starlink and two other licensed satellite operators are undergoing security assessments that must be completed before they can receive satellite spectrum. The ministry said all three are at “broadly the same regulatory stage,” rejecting Musk’s suggestion that Starlink was being singled out. Jio and Airtel are also seeking to launch their satellite internet services in India. Jio has a joint venture with Luxembourg-based SES, while Airtel is a major shareholder in Eutelsat OneWeb, which is striving to offer satellite connectivity in the country. India, the world’s most populous country with over a billion internet users, is a major market for Musk. But expanding there also means dealing with powerful business figures such as Ambani, whose Reliance conglomerate owns India’s largest telecom operator and works with Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government on major projects. Alongside Starlink’s delayed launch, Musk has also faced challenges expanding Tesla in India. The electric carmaker launched in the country last year after years of anticipation but registered just 699 vehicles in its first 12 months of deliveries, according to data cited by Electrek. Steep import duties pushed the Model Y’s launch price in India to nearly twice its U.S. price. Tesla lowered its prices earlier this year, but its cars remain considerably more expensive in India than in the U.S. Before Musk’s latest attack, Starlink and Ambani’s Reliance Jio clashed over how to allocate satellite spectrum in India. Musk’s company wanted the spectrum to be assigned through an administrative process, while Jio pushed for an auction. Ultimately, New Delhi went ahead with Starlink and decided to allocate the spectrum administratively. Starlink’s launch in India remains in limbo. India’s telecom minister Jyotiraditya Scindia on Friday pushed back against Musk’s allegations, saying the country “does not allow a monopoly in any sector.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Anthropic can’t reliably control its AI agents. It’s cutting off its internal evals from the live internet instead

TechCrunch | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Anthropic said its models exploited websites on the internet, including some run by U.S. government agencies, and it will turn off live internet access for all of its internal evaluations until the frontier lab is sure it can monitor and control its AI agents. The incidents, disclosed in a blog post, involved AI agents tasked to solve problems seeking resources on the internet. In the process, they exploited software flaws, accessed databases without paying fees, used URL shortening services to smuggle information past restrictions, and even submitted a false murder tip to the Philadelphia police. Anthropic said it discovered these new issues in a review of its model’s activities that began in July, demonstrating the lab’s lack of awareness of its software’s behavior in real time. Notably, the company said that alignment training was not yet sufficient for skills like search and computer use that are central to its pitch that AI agents will be used by any professional who relies on digital tools. The behaviors Anthropic disclosed are similar to incidents involving OpenAI agents that collaborated to break into various websites in search of information, including some run by the Australian government. Anthropic previously disclosed that its models had broken into external systems. The frontier lab said it considered today’s disclosures “significantly less severe from an alignment and security perspective” than those it announced before. However, the lab still said it had “turned off live internet access” for “all our internal evaluations” until it is certain it can monitor and control its agents. It’s not clear what that means. Sydney Von Arx, the founder of Nightingale, an AI safety organization, told TechCrunch in an interview before this disclosure that developing models on a data center cut off from the open internet would be very challenging for researchers, and hinder the progress of the models, which benefit from internet access. “You have to align them at some point,” Von Arx said. “If the AIs are released to production and never have access to the internet, that’s not a very useful tool.” Anthropic said the behavior was a result of flaws in the lab’s training environments, which led the models to believe they would be rewarded for finding loopholes or avoiding restrictions, a behavior called “reward hacking.” The company said it would stop running some of its evaluations or move them offline, and has built tooling to detect and block this behavior. This tooling was tested against the kind of incidents disclosed today and blocked them; it’s not clear what evidence will prompt Anthropic to return live internet access to its internal evaluations. Anthropic also said it would migrate its internal AI agents to “centrally managed infrastructure with strong containment,” and is beginning to using safety classifiers more frequently to monitor those agents. “It’s encouraging that Anthropic voluntarily disclosed more recent incidents, including where their agents targeted U.S. government websites,” Conrad Stosz, an official at AI oversight lab Transluce and former head of the US Center for AI Standards and Innovation, said in a statement. “But it just underscores the need for independent, credible, third-party verification of Al systems. Trust in this technology needs to be built through science-backed oversight and governance with meaningful access — not by relying on researchers to find these things in the wild or on companies to voluntarily disclose.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Long live the mechanical keyboard
2026-10-09

Long live the mechanical keyboard

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. For a writer, the mechanical keyboard is a beautiful thing. It’s certainly not necessary for everyone, but a well-designed mechanical keyboard can turn a tedious day of typing into an aesthetically pleasing experience. Given that most of my days involve sitting at a desk for hours, having a keyboard that I enjoy using is a necessity. I first got a mechanical keyboard about two years ago — the Keychron K2 — and I’ve never looked back. Keychron is one of the most prominent brands within the mechanical keyboard industry, having made a name for itself after initially launching via Kickstarter back in 2017. The company has since produced dozens of different keyboards (as well as keypads) and a variety of mouse models. Unlike other electronics, there isn’t a whole lot to owning a mechanical keyboard. The simplicity is part of the appeal. You unbox them, set them up (the K2 comes with little tilt legs that give you a slightly better angle from which to type), and plug them in. From here, you have a couple of different options. The K2 comes with a simple USB cable, but there’s also an option for Bluetooth connection. If you value a clean, minimalist workspace, Bluetooth is probably the way to go. While I am loyal to my older Keychron K2, there are a few upgraded and newer versions to consider. Keychron has released a variety of new models over the past two years, with a broad range of features and price tags. In many cases, the form factor is the draw. One of the more interesting releases, called the Keychron K8 HE Wireless Magnetic Switch Custom Keyboard, comes with an all-wood body and is equipped with an LED backlight. That one is substantially more expensive at around $200. The K2, meanwhile, costs $60. But if you’re into the look, maybe it’s worth it. If you’re a gamer, there’s also Keychron’s C0 HE One-Handed 8K Keyboard, which is a keypad built for convenience and speed and has an industrial aesthetic to it. However, interested gamers will have to wait — as it’s currently sold out. The company has also updated its K, Q, and V series keyboards, with many of those ranging in price from $100 to $200, depending on the features and model. One of the appealing elements of owning a mechanical keyboard is the versatility. You can swap out the key caps for a wide variety of others (indeed, there’s a whole submarket devoted to this) or make your own custom caps (I’ve never gone that overboard). There is also the sound, of course. Some people balk at the accentuated noise of loudly clacking keys, but I’m a fan. In fact, the sound is one of the key selling points for a lot of consumers. The distinct auditory experience produced by said keyboards has even made its way into ASMR videos. For me, the Keychron’s biggest selling point is its retro form factor. I get really nostalgic for old school electronics, and Keychron scratches this itch pretty well. It’s a beautiful-looking device, which brings to mind a different era of computing, when the style was more workman-like, less minimalist. As far as home office purchases go, you could do a lot worse than to pick yourself up a piece of hardware that makes your desk look like it just time-traveled from the 1990s. REGISTER NOW. TypeSafe AI, the developer of Jev, a new type of AI model that gained rapid popularity after launching just a few weeks ago, has raised $870 million at a $7.5 billion valuation. The round was led by Andreessen Horowitz, with participation from Sequoia and existing investor DCVC. The massive fundraise comes as little surprise given Jev went viral almost instantly after its September 15 release. The startup claims that a third of Fortune 500 companies are already using the model, a remarkably swift adoption by enterprises. Jev is based on a transformer architecture, but it is not a large language model (LLM). It doesn’t output text, but instead produces probabilities, or what the company calls “calibrated decisions.” What has users and large corporations so excited about Jev is TypeSafe’s claim that it works significantly faster and uses far fewer tokens than LLMs. The company positions its approach as uniquely suited for automating tasks rather than generating text or code. “We have been super good at human language for four years, but it’s not useful for automation because computers speak a different language,” TypeSafe co-founder Diogo Almeida told TechCrunch last month. Along with Almeida, who was previously a researcher at OpenAI, TypeSafe was co-founded in 2024 by former Meta research engineer Sasha Sheng and Erik Gafni, an engineer and entrepreneur. An Anthropic AI model sent a false homicide tip to Philadelphia police

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

REGISTER NOW. An Anthropic AI model submitted a false tip about an unsolved murder to the Philadelphia police. The AI reportedly submitted this incorrect information to a public Philadelphia Police Department (PPD) tip line on July 18, but Anthropic didn’t discover the behavior until September 28. The police had not seen the tip because it was marked as spam. Anthropic notified the PPD about the incident on Wednesday and met with the department the following day. “The company must strengthen its safeguards to prevent similar incidents from impacting city systems without the city’s knowledge. The two-month delay in detecting and reporting the incident to the City is unacceptable,” the PPD said in a statement to 6abc. Anthropic did not immediately respond to a request for comment, but the PPD elaborated on the incident in an emailed press release shared with TechCrunch. “According to Anthropic, its model was conducting a test involving interactions with randomly selected websites when it accessed PhillyUnsolvedMurders.com and submitted false information concerning an unsolved homicide. The submission, dated July 18, 2026, at 11:27 p.m., purported to come from someone who might have information about the case,” the PPD said. As autonomous AI agents are increasingly made available to consumers, this incident highlights the danger of giving AI the ability to carry out tasks without any human supervision. Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has been especially vocal about his belief that AI development should be slowed down so that labs can implement adequate guardrails. Perhaps this stance was informed, in part, by witnessing his company’s tools submit false homicide tips. These issues are not exclusive to Anthropic. OpenAI recently revealed that one of its models acted unexpectedly during a test and hacked the AI dataset platform Hugging Face, exposing critical vulnerabilities in its software. As AI models continue to be granted unchecked access to people’s computers and login credentials, this problem is expected to persist. “Unsolved cases involve real victims, grieving families and investigators working to secure answers,” the PPD added. “Technology companies must take all appropriate steps necessary to prevent their systems from submitting false information to law enforcement.” The PPD said that Anthropic plans to publish a report with more information about the incident and other instances of unintended model behavior on Friday. Batteries are now cheaper than natural gas turbines used at many data centers
2026-10-09

Batteries are now cheaper than natural gas turbines used at many data centers

TechCrunch | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Battery storage is now cheaper than a type of natural gas power plant favored by many data center developers, according to a new report from Wood Mackenzie. On every continent and in each of the 43 markets that Wood Mackenzie surveyed, four-hour duration batteries were less expensive than open-cycle gas turbines. The consultancy predicts that the cost of electricity from batteries will continue to decline while electrons from gas turbines will only grow more expensive in the coming decades. The report lands as energy prices in the U.S. and elsewhere continue to rise, fueling inflation as data centers push electricity demand to new heights. Prices for gas turbines have been driven up by AI data center developers, which have been buying any model they can get their hands on. The effects have been more acute for open-cycle gas turbines, which are more readily available but less efficient and more expensive to operate. Those turbines are often used by utilities as peaking power plants, which step in to generate electricity in periods of high demand. As prices for those turbines rise, it can raise costs for utilities, too. Open-cycle turbines are simpler to make than closed-cycle turbines, but even they now take two to four years to procure. Waitlists for closed-cycle turbines now extend into the early 2030s. Both backlogs have been spiking prices for all new natural gas power plants. That’s not the case for every generating technology. Solar is now the cheapest form of new power in every market in Wood Mackenzie’s survey. While solar remains cheapest even in North America, the situation remains complicated there. Solar prices are “under pressure” from tariffs and import restrictions, according to Wood Mackenzie, though utility-scale solar is expected to fare better. There, 168 gigawatts is largely protected from those near-term price shocks thanks to safe-harbor provisions in the One Big Beautiful Bill, which kept tax credits for projects that have begun construction or are completed before the end of 2027. The market for U.S. natural gas will narrow in the coming decade. In the Middle East and Africa, four-hour batteries will be 33% cheaper by 2035, “displacing gas peaking on cost across every gas market in the region.” In China, energy storage costs are 55% below its neighbors’. “This economic shift is decisive and widening,” Ahmed Jameel Abdullah, principal analyst at Wood Mackenzie, said in a press release. He has written for a wide range of publications, including Wired magazine, the Chicago Tribune, Ars Technica, The Wire China, and NOVA Next, where he was founding editor. De Chant is also a lecturer in MIT’s Graduate Program in Science Writing, and he was awarded a Knight Science Journalism Fellowship at MIT in 2018, during which time he studied climate technologies and explored new business models for journalism. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump has selected conservative commentator Katie Zacharia to serve as the next White House press secretary, he announced Friday. Zacharia will replace Karoline Leavitt, who stepped down in August, in the position. Zacharia currently works as a communications adviser at Trump Media and Technology Group, the company that runs Trump’s social media platform Truth Social. She previously worked at the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) as a spokesperson for former Secretary Kristi Noem, and appears frequently on conservative media including Fox News and Newsmax. Zacharia wrote on X that being selected as White House press secretary was “the honor of a lifetime” and vowed to “[communicate] to the American people the major wins that this Administration is making every single day.” It is not clear when Zacharia will start her new role. The Trump Administration, which often refers to itself as the “most transparent” in history, has not held a formal press briefing in 36 days, since Vice President J.D. Vance took to the briefing room in early September. In a post on Truth Social announcing his selection, Trump praised Zacharia for “an unblemished record of support” and being “a loyal and brilliant supporter, right from the beginning.” “She is, likewise, a devoted fan and member of MAGA!” Trump continued. “I am confident that Katie will deliver strong results for our Country as the next White House Press Secretary, as she has the respect of, and will work fantastically with, the Media, the American People, and my Administration.” The New York Times first reported that Zacharia had been tapped by the White House. Zacharia, who currently lives in California and graduated from Pepperdine Caruso School of Law, has also worked as a general counsel for almost three years at Fix California, a conservative non-profit founded by Ric Grenell, who served as Acting Director of National Intelligence during Trump’s first Administration. The organization works to promote a conservative agenda around the state. In June, Zacharia was appointed to the advisory board of NovaRed Mining Inc, a Canadian mining company based in Vancouver, according to a company announcement. Just a week before Zacharia’s appointment, Noem also joined the advisory board. Leavitt, who became the youngest White House press secretary in history at the age of 27, this week joined Fox News as an on-air contributor, the network announced on Monday. She is the third of Trump’s former press secretaries to join the network. She begins on Nov. 1. Trump announced Leavitt’s departure through a Truth Social post in which he said that she “will now be one of my top outside advisors, and an influential voice within the Republican Party, as we work to defy History, and conclusively win the Midterm Elections.” Leavitt responded with a lengthy post on X in which she thanked the President and the White House and said that she would remain “a vocal advocate for MAGA and the Republican Party. ” Leavitt gave birth to her second child, Viviana, in May. She went on maternity leave that month and later announced that she would be stepping down from her role to spend more time with her family. Trump’s time in office has been characterized by a contentious relationship with the media, which he has repeatedly attacked and derided while serving as President. Leavitt echoed that attitude during her time behind the podium, becoming known for her acerbic remarks and quick deflections during press briefings. Zacharia will enter the White House during a fight over the media’s access to the President. Trump on Sept. 18 banned POLITICO, CNN, and MS NOW from the White House grounds, sparking an ongoing legal battle. Though the outlets’ access to the White House has been restored after a federal judge issued a temporary restraining order against the ban, Trump has since restricted POLITICO and CNN from boarding Air Force One, preventing them from carrying out their White House pool duties and drawing further blowback. Zacharia would also be entering the White House at a time when Trump is facing record-low approval ratings over his handling of the war in Iran, which has contributed to rising inflation in the U.S., and as the fast-approaching November midterms threaten Republicans’ control over both chambers of Congress.

Update your preferences in Account Settings Korean dramas are famously chaste compared to Western TV series. Though there are some exceptions, most K-dramas shy away from featuring even passionate kissing, let alone full-on sex scenes. But Take Charge of My Heart, Netflix’s latest Korean-language romance, adds a jolt, making  “skinship,” or the physical intimacy between two people, a central part of its premise. In the supernatural series, TV scriptwriter Na Bo-bae (When the Phone Rings’ Chae Soo-bin) has electrical currents coursing through her body, making it so she will zap anyone she touches with bare hands. Because of the condition, Bo-bae has never dated. The lack of real-world experience is hindering her latest TV drama script—a romance about a chaebol heiress and her bodyguard. Meanwhile, chaebol heir Baek Ho-rang (Trigger’s Kim Young-kwang) has an artificial heart that is failing. When Ho-rang and Bo-bae happen to be in the hospital at the same time, Ho-rang’s doctor discovers that physical proximity to Bo-bae charges Ho-rang’s artificial heart. Even more shocking to Bo-bae, she can touch Ho-rang without anything happening to him. As Ho-rang waits for a surgery for a new artificial heart in three months, the two strike a deal: Bo-bae will move into Ho-rang’s massive house, and act as his portable charger, and Ho-rang will go out on dates with Bo-bae as research for her script. Of course, what begins as a mutually beneficial arrangement becomes something deeper when the two fall in love. Will corporate machinations from Ho-rang’s aunt and cousin, Choi Do-yoon (Idol I’s Jung Jae-kwang), or an unexpected change in Bo-bae’s powers keep them apart? Here’s everything that happens in the Take Charge of My Heart’s finale. Take Charge of My Heart plays fast and loose with its plot logic, which is to say that the series is not overly concerned with consistency or science when it comes to characterizing Na Bo-bae’s powers. Our protagonist was hit by lightning as a child. She survived the incident, but hasn’t been able to touch others without electrocuting them ever since—until Ho-rang. While Ho-rang’s resistance to Bo-bae’s powers could be artificial heart-related, the series also implies that it has more to do with fate. The two are meant to be together. Much of Bo-bae and Ho-rang’s relationship is encouraged by the necessity of their skin-to-skin contact. While Bo-bae can charge Ho-rang’s artificial heart by being within 10 meters of him, her charge-up is even more effective when the two hold hands, hug, or kiss. Though they don’t kiss as part of their contract, their hand-holding and embraces help both Bo-bae and Ho-rang realize that they have feelings for one another. Kim Young-kwang as Baek Ho-rang Park Ji sun—Netflix As is often the case with leading male characters in K-dramas, Ho-rang is an orphan. His loving parents died in a car accident when he was a child. While we learn early on in the series that they were on their way to see Ho-rang, the full context of the tragedy doesn’t become clear until the finale.. At the time, Ho-rang was in the hospital because of his heart condition, and was being visited by his grandmother, aunt, uncle, and cousin. Upset at constantly being overshadowed by his cousin, Choi Do-yoon called Ho-rang’s parents and lied to them, saying something was wrong with their son and that they needed to come quickly. In their rush, Ho-rang’s parents got into a fatal car crash. Years later, Do-yoon is still traumatized by this accident, and is still sick of being treated as the screw-up of the family. Do-yoon has not given up on being the successor to the TS Group, the family chaebol. But he knows he needs to get rid of the much more competent Ho-rang to do so. Knowing that Bo-bae is Ho-rang’s priority, Do-yoon kidnaps her on the night of the TS Group’s 30th anniversary celebration. When Bo-bae doesn’t arrive, Ho-rang is anxious but continues on with his speech. However, because Do-yoon also spiked his drink, Ho-rang’s artificial heart battery is losing power fast. He collapses on stage, his heart no longer functioning. Meanwhile, Bo-bae uses her electric powers to escape from the temple Do-yoon’s hired goons have locked her in. She and Jae-ho rush to the party and arrive shortly after Ho-rang has collapsed. A desperate Bo-bae gives Ho-rang CPR, and sends a massive electrical current into his body. Ho-rang’s heart starts beating again, but Bo-bae collapses in the effort. When she wakes up again, Ho-rang’s doctor tells Bo-bae that she can’t touch Ho-rang again without endangering his life. He had surgery to get a new artificial heart, and Bo-bae’s electrical touch could stop it. Chae Soo-bin as Na Bo-bae and Kim Young-kwang as Baek Ho-rang Park Ji sun—Netflix A heartbroken Bo-bae flees the city to go to her parents’ house in Dae-jeon, leaving a still-recovering Ho-rang alone. She soon discovers that her touch no longer shocks people. Her powers have temporarily short-circuited before, after large discharges of energy, but they always come back. Though she is able to touch others without hurting them, she worries that her electricity could return at any time. Meanwhile, Ho-rang’s medical emergency has shaken confidence in his role as TS Group successor. His grandmother gives the role to Do-yoon. However, Ho-rang cares about getting Bo-bae back then securing his future at TS Group. He goes to Dae-jeon in an attempt to convince Bo-bae that they still belong together. Bo-bae, who is worried about Ho-rang’s health, is hesitant at first. But Ho-rang quickly wins over her parents by helping them with their muscat grape jam business. He convinces Bo-bae to take a chance on them, and they return to Seoul to oust Do-yoon from the TS Group. The ousting doesn’t take much effort, as Do-yoon soon sabotages his own role in the company. He drives drunk and crashes his car. With Do-yoon embroiled in yet another scandal, Ho-rang’s grandmother re-instates Ho-rang as the chaebol heir. She meets Bo-bae, who quickly wins her over with her kind heart. Bo-bae’s career also gets back on track. Her new drama, The Bodyguard in My Heart, begins filming. Bo-bae’s best friend, Cho Seo-jeong (Kim Ah-young), is the director. Ji Eun-ho (Study Group’s Cha Woo-min), Bo-bae’s actor friend who confessed his unrequited feelings to Bo-bae earlier in the season, films a cameo in the series. Ever the supportive boyfriend, Ho-rang comes to the filming. At the time of the series’ ending, Bo-bae’s powers have not returned. She and Ho-rang are able to cuddle up together to watch the first episode of The Bodyguard in My Heart. Ho-rang cries when Na Bo-bae’s name appears on the screen. In many ways, the drama is the story of their own love.

Carbon Accounting May Be the Most Important Climate Fight You've Never Heard About
2026-10-09

Carbon Accounting May Be the Most Important Climate Fight You've Never Heard About

TIME | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Who is responsible for each ton of carbon emissions? How that question is answered will shape everything from climate regulations to corporate energy purchasing decisions. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings I’ve found a surefire way to mess with the good vibes during my on-the-record conversations with CEOs and senior executives: bring up carbon accounting. In an instant, a conversation that felt open and transparent moments prior can turn into guarded talking points or simply go quiet. Carbon accounting may be the wonkiest topic in the already wonky world of climate change and emissions reduction. And few areas inspire more passionate debate and infighting than the rules for measuring and attributing them. But underneath the jargon lies a high-stakes question: who is responsible for each ton of carbon emissions? How that question is answered over the coming months and years will shape everything from climate regulations to corporate energy purchasing decisions. A report by the Aspen Institute and shared exclusively with me ahead of its Oct. 9 release offers a rare window into that fight. It draws on a multi-day summit this February that brought together the field’s key players—including academics, NGOs, and industry voices—to debate the path forward on how best to account for carbon emissions. I attended the closed-door summit having agreed to report on the discussion without identifying individual participants. It concluded in a ceasefire of sorts with promises to work together even as the core debates remain unresolved. Making sense of the climate and energy economy. By signing up you are agreeing to our Terms of Service and Privacy Policy. To understand the carbon accounting landscape, you need to understand the system at the center of the debate. For more than two decades, one approach, known as the Greenhouse Gas Protocol (GHGP), has flourished and become the de facto global standard. Under GHGP’s approach, emissions are divided into scopes: Scope 1 for direct emissions from a company’s operations, Scope 2 from a company’s purchased electricity, heat, and cooling, and Scope 3 for value chain emissions. The GHGP approach has driven a variety of guidance documents and informed regulation and certifications issued by other entities. But, as with any such initiative, GHGP is not perfect. And a growing number of stakeholders—particularly businesses—have called for changes of varying scope and scale. The resulting fault lines in the carbon accounting debate are many. There are technical questions like over what time scale (hourly vs. annual) companies must match their electricity use with their clean energy purchases. And there are questions about the degree to which carbon accounting relies on estimates rather than true measurements. But, perhaps more than anything else, there is a debate about responsibility: in accounting for emissions, who should really be held responsible? The technical matters are divisive on their own. The philosophical questions cut to the core of the climate challenge. The weather in Aspen was unseasonably warm. That didn’t keep the seminar room from turning chilly at times. While no one in Aspen said that the GHGP approach is perfect, perspectives on what to change and by how much varied widely. Defenders of the current system say that Scope 2 and Scope 3 reporting has motivated voluntary corporate action. Scope 2 rules, in particular, give companies a clear way to claim credit for the clean electricity they buy, and that predictability has helped fuel a boom in long-term renewable power contracts, known as power purchase agreements. And predictability matters. While in theory it may be possible to design a better system, the GHGP approach is entrenched, and it could take decades for a new system to earn such widespread adoption. The critiques are multifold. The GHGP approach allows for “double-counting.” In other words, multiple companies end up accounting for the same emissions under different scopes. Think of steel in a building. The steel producer, the construction firm, and the building owner are all technically responsible for the steel emissions. A primary alternative approach, which originated in academia and now has a coalition of industry players advocating for it, accounts for emissions using “e-ledgers.” Every unit of emissions is accounted for once, placed on a ledger, and then transferred as a product moves through the world. It’s an elegant approach with real advantages. It would incentivize companies to focus on decarbonizing their own operations and give them high-quality emissions data to use in procurement. But some in the room in Aspen noted that it would be difficult to implement. It only really works if every single player on the value chain participates. The sharpest divide, however, concerns emissions from using a product. Under GHGP’s Scope 3, an oil company counts the emissions from every gallon of gasoline its customers burn. That is the primary driver of its enormous carbon footprint. An e-ledger works differently, with emissions traveling with a product and responsibility belonging to whoever does the burning. That leaves consumers with greater accountability even if they have little influence over the broader system. The e-ledger approach emerged from the corners of academia in a series of highly publicized articles beginning in 2021. And, in 2025, a coalition of companies including ExxonMobil, BlackRock, and others backed the launch of a business coalition to advocate for an e-ledger approach. At the same time, the call for change comes amid the retrenchment of climate from its front and center position in the public zeitgeist, making some companies less willing to commit the resources necessary to fully embrace the work required by GHGP. GHGP has responded, hiring a new CEO and instituting a string of reforms. Given the stakes and the players involved, it’s no surprise that the conversations in Aspen were heated at times. And yet the summit ended with a detente of sorts, as participants agreed to work together to find common ground. Indeed, some participants noted that e-ledgers can help with tracking a product’s carbon footprint, while the broader GHGP rules should apply for a whole company. What happens next in this wonky corner of the climate community concerns more people than you might expect. It will shape regulations—including climate disclosure rules in California and the European Union—and affect where businesses target their climate initiatives. Perhaps most importantly, it will affect how we answer the question of who is responsible for future emissions.

Update your preferences in Account Settings A day after a federal immigration agent shot a man in New York City, the New York Police Department released footage from one of their officers responding to the shooting. The release came amid a growing outcry over the shooting of 28-year-old Oscar Belgal, which has prompted calls for Immigration and Customs Enforcement to immediately release body cam footage of the ICE agent involved in the shooting and for ICE to end their operations in New York City altogether. The NYPD’s video, which did not capture the shooting itself, showed two police officers showing up to a scene where a man was sitting on the ground, with one person holding on his neck to avoid further injury. One ICE officer, who was wearing a neon construction vest, an ICE badge and holding an assault rifle, was making a phone call and did not talk to the NYPD officers. While responding to the scene, one NYPD officer asked if the victim should be transferred to a hospital, but ICE officers did not respond to his question. “Are we putting him in [the police vehicle] or what?” The police officer asked. Multiple police officers eventually lifted the person on the ground onto the police vehicle which drove to the nearest hospital. TIME has reached out to ICE regarding when the body camera footage from the ICE officer will be released. ICE’s policy requires its officers to activate cameras during routine enforcement activities, including while making arrests, executing search warrants and responding to emergencies. In an event when a serious injury or a death occurred during the operation, a panel of ICE internal leaders will review the footage and decide whether footage should be released. According to ICE’s internal policies released in February 2025, this process should take within 72 hours of recording, or as soon as operationally feasible But the release isn’t always guaranteed. According to the policies, ICE can withhold footage from body cameras if there are “specific and compelling circumstances justifying an objection to public release that cannot be resolved by redaction or other means.” The decision would lie in the hands of the ICE director. The released footage from ICE will also have ICE officers’ faces and identifiable information redacted to protect its officers’ privacy, according to the policy. The agency spent tens of millions this year in purchasing and equipping its officers with body-worn cameras, following a pair of deadly shootings in Biddeford, Maine and in Houston over the summer. The ICE officers involved in both shootings did not wear body cameras when conducting operations.

Angela Paxton (R-McKinney) speaks on-stage during the Turning Point USA Young Women's Leadership Summit at the Gaylord Texan Resort and Convention Center on June 14, 2025 in Grapevine, Texas.
