Update your preferences in Account Settings Angela Paxton, the estranged wife of Texas Attorney General and Republican Senate candidate Ken Paxton, has waded into the state’s hotly contested Senate race by encouraging Texans to vote Republican in the midterm elections—while avoiding directly mentioning her husband. Angela Paxton—who filed for divorce last year, alleging adultery—long remained mum on the race, in which her husband is facing off against Democrat James Talarico. Over the summer, she declined to share with the New York Times whether she would vote for Ken Paxton in the November election. But as Talarico continues to hold a slim lead over her husband in most polls and Election Day draws closer, she has been speaking out to urge voters to back the GOP when they head to the voting booths next month. In a speech to a group of Republican women in mid-September, she said that the Republican Party has “some vulnerabilities on our ballot,” but urged them to still vote for the party’s candidates “even if we’re mad at it.” “I’ll be honest with you, I think Republican women will decide this election, because some of those vulnerabilities, I think, have been particularly alienating to women that hold our values,” she said. “When I say we have to win, I mean we have to win all those races. We have to keep all of those seats.” “It’s not the people—it’s the seat,” she continued. “Because we have more control over that seat and the future when we control the seat, when it’s still in the family, even if we’re mad at it. But if we lose it to the other team, the nuances that we want to fix with maybe a certain candidate are nothing compared to what it’s going to take to get it back.” Read More: How Trump Helped Put a Texas Senate Seat In Play And she is now sharing a similar message in a new ad, in which she talks about, in her own words, “the elephant in the room.” “People have asked my thoughts about this election and it turns out, I have some. Let’s talk about the elephant in the room,” Angela Paxton opens the ad by saying. “In a general election, it’s not as much about the individual players on the team—it’s about getting the whole team across the finish line,” she says. “Either we hold every Republican seat or we hand the future of Texas to the other team. That’s why I’m voting for the Republican ticket on Nov. 3. Let’s hold the line together.” She does not speak about Ken Paxton or the Senate contest outright in the ad. The ad was funded by Texas PAC, which is tied to a super PAC that backs a Republican majority in the upper chamber. Here’s what to know about Angela Paxton, and the significance of her wading into the midterm elections. Angela Paxton, a Republican, has served in the Texas state Senate since 2019. She was previously a math teacher and school counselor. She and Ken Paxton have been married for nearly four decades, but last year, she announced that she filed for divorce “on biblical grounds,” stating that, “in light of recent discoveries, I do not believe that it honors God or is loving to myself, my children, or Ken to remain in the marriage.” In her divorce petition, Angela Paxton alleged that her husband “has committed adultery” and said that the two of them had stopped living together “as spouses” by June 2024. A spokesperson for the Texas state senator told the Times over the weekend that the divorce proceedings are still underway. Political experts tell TIME that the ad featuring Angela Paxton suggests that the Republican Party is worried about her husband’s chances of winning the Senate race. “The fact that the need was felt to reach out to [Texas state senator Angela Paxton] to obliquely vouch for her now estranged husband is pretty remarkable, and an indication of the extent of concern that Republicans are feeling in this race,” says Joshua Blank, the research director of the Texas Politics Project at the University of Texas at Austin. Most polls show that Paxton is trailing—by a very slim margin—behind Talarico. If Talarico wins the Senate race, it would be the first time that a Democratic candidate has won a statewide election in Texas in more than three decades. Paxton’s campaign has been shadowed by several scandals and controversies that have arisen throughout his political career, including allegations of infidelity, a fraud indictment, and an impeachment trial on charges of bribery and abuse of office. Paxton has denied any wrongdoing. The fraud charges against him were ultimately dropped after prosecutors and Paxton agreed to a deal, and he was acquitted of all charges against him in his impeachment trial by the Texas state Senate. Political experts say that President Donald Trump’s low approval ratings—particularly how voters feel about his handling of the economy—could also be impacting Paxton’s performance in the polls, as well as an unusually tight gubernatorial race in Texas. “Nationwide, we know it’s a difficult environment for Republicans. That’s playing out in Texas too, though Texas is more Republican than most states and so there’s more of a buffer,” says Kirby Goidel, a professor of political science at Texas A&M University. According to data shared by CNN Chief Data Analyst Harry Enten on air earlier this month, Paxton’s net popularity is the lowest among several Republicans vying for key Senate seats next month. “[Paxton is] in a tight race like other Republicans, but he’s in a tighter race in a worse situation because, I think, of the baggage that he brings to the campaign,” Goidel says. Goidel says that, if you’re the Republican Party looking at these polls, “you say, what does Ken Paxton have to do to win this race?” “Well, the first thing he has to do is secure Republican support,” Goidel continues. “I think having his wife come on and send that message that this is the team game, not an individual game, and we need all the players on the field, is a … signal [from the party of] ‘Hey, we need you all to come on board and vote for this guy; you may not like it, but we need you.’” Blank also says that the ad featuring Angela Paxton “is not so indirectly addressed at giving women, but in particular Republican women who might have apprehensions about supporting Ken Paxton, with permission to do so.” A New York Times/Siena University poll conducted at the end of September found that more men who are likely voters in Texas said they would vote for Paxton than women who are likely voters in the state, at 51% compared to 40%. Blank points out, though, that there’s a gender gap in partisanship in American politics more generally—women tend to be more likely than men to align themselves with the Democratic Party, while men tend to be more likely than women to align themselves with the Republican Party. Blank notes that “polling has indicated a consistent gap in enthusiasm among Republican voters.” “Republican women in particular might be a soft spot in the coalition, and if anything, this ad indicates that that’s likely the case,” Blank says.

Update your preferences in Account Settings Bruce Springsteen, Tom Morello, Joan Baez, and others appear onstage for the finale of the 2026 Power To The People Festival on Oct. 03, 2026 in Columbia, Md. Update your preferences in Account Settings Tom Morello has been staging benefit shows since before Rage Against the Machine made him an icon: $10 at the door, proceeds to a homeless shelter, guitars loud enough to wage a sonic revolt against the establishment. Last year, while serving as musical director of Back to the Beginning, the Birmingham farewell concert for Ozzy Osbourne and Black Sabbath, he began imagining something bigger: a multigenerational, multigenre festival built on his foundational proposition that music can be an act of resistance—aimed squarely at Donald Trump and the forces he has unleashed. "It's no accident that the concert was about three weeks out from the midterms," Morello told me after the Oct. 3 Power to the People show at Merriweather Post Pavilion in Columbia, Md., less than 30 minutes from the capital whose balance of power those elections will decide. "In a time where hatred and division are rife, this is a festival of love and solidarity. In a time where injustice is rampant, this is a festival celebrating those who are standing up against injustice. And in a time where artists are either being canceled or censoring themselves, it was a guarantee the artists were going to be able to play what they wanted to play and say what they wanted to say." To that end, Morello assembled a sprawling roster of rebels and rock-n-roll royalty: Joan Baez and Public Enemy; Jack Black and the Dropkick Murphys; Cypress Hill and Nathaniel Rateliff; Killer Mike and Serj Tankian; Stephen Stills and Dave Matthews and the Foo Fighters. Headlining the event was Bruce Springsteen, fresh off a 20-show tour that had become a nightly rebuke of Trump, MAGA, and the authoritarian currents he saw threatening the American experiment. Power to the People suggested a rebellious energy stirring at a moment when many artists had grown wary of speaking out, fearful of backlash, of alienating part of their audience, of the wrath of an administration that has repeatedly targeted prominent critics. "I feel as though we're trying to turn the tide and we're facing a tsunami," said Baez, who sang alongside Bob Dylan and Pete Seeger during the protest movements of the 1960s and '70s. Then she turned to the crowd. "You are the antidote in a world of cruelty and bullying." With Rateliff beside her, she launched into a haunting rendition of Woody Guthrie's "Deportee." Inevitably, the place felt like a progressive family reunion: aging boomers who carry the counterculture as a kind of secular inheritance; younger activists animated by rage against corporate power, democratic erosion, and the war in Gaza; keffiyehs, protest shirts, and homemade placards scattered across the grounds. One read, Keep the Immigrants. Deport the Racists. MS NOW livestreamed the proceedings. It would be easy to dismiss the show as an exercise in preaching to the converted. But movements have to begin somewhere, and politics is downstream of culture. If the message travels beyond the lawn, Power to the People may come to represent one of the seminal cultural events of the era: a gathering of artists, at a liminal moment in American life, turning their influence into a political insurgency to preserve the nation's democratic future. A shot of the stage at the Power to the People Festival Paul Morigi—Getty Images Morello had been planning the festival for more than a year, but he says the "turning point" came after the Operation Metro Surge, when Trump administration deployed roughly 3,000 federal officers to Minneapolis this winter for the largest immigration-enforcement operation in the country, which culminated in the killings of two Americans, Renee Good and Alex Pretti. "When you're putting on a show like this, managers and agents are not always eager to get involved," Morello told me. But after Minneapolis, something changed. "Artists started calling me up and raising their hand, saying, 'I understand you're putting on a festival. Can I be involved?'" One was Ken Casey, frontman of the Dropkick Murphys, who tore through a 12-song set that ended with the band's signature anthem, "I'm Shipping Up to Boston," built around lyrics by Guthrie. "Days like today let us know that there's a community out there that wants to see a peaceful America, an empathetic America, a unified America, that wants to see control of our government given back to the people," Casey told me. The concert began at one in the afternoon and ran for 10 hours, the history of American protest music compressed into a single day. During Morello's early-evening set, Tankian from System of a Down joined him for Audioslave's "Like a Stone," a tribute to Morello's late bandmate Chris Cornell. Macklemore, recently dropped as an opener on Ed Sheeran's tour because of pressure from New England Patriots owner Robert Kraft over the artist’s pro-Palestinian advocacy, had been invited but was unavailable. So he sent a video message that played on the giant screens. The controversy itself reinforced the festival's defiant argument—rebelling against a world where speaking out still carries a price. The lineage grew explicit as the night wore on. Baez, Rateliff, and Matthews joined for "Deportee," then moved into the civil-rights standard "Ain't Gonna Let Nobody Turn Me Around." As they left the stage, Stephen Stills appeared and reached back to another era of upheaval with Buffalo Springfield's "For What It's Worth." Cypress Hill followed, then Public Enemy with their landmark "Fight the Power," then Dave Matthews. The Foo Fighters opened their set with "Times Like These," a song born of uncertainty and near-fracture for the band that transformed into an anthem of renewal. They closed with "Everlong." a track about the desire to make a perfect moment last forever, despite knowing that it can’t. But the concert’s climax was Springsteen's set. At 76, he summoned the power and energy that have defined him over a half century, as he occupies yet another role now, too: the elder statesman among a generation of musicians deciding how much to risk by taking a stand. "When you have a guy that's at that stage of his life, he's done all the stuff, he's comfortable, he didn't have to go on the road and do that and put the crosshairs on himself, but he did it anyway," Casey said. "If artists can't take a lead from that, then my God, nobody's paying attention." Springsteen opened with "Streets of Minneapolis," his protest song against Operation Metro Surge, written days after ICE flooded Minnesota's streets and murdered two protestors. He then brought Baez onstage for a searing acoustic rendition of Dylan’s "Chimes of Freedom.” From there the set erupted into anthemic rock fueled by righteous anger. He ripped through the Clash's "Clampdown" with Morello, then "The Ghost of Tom Joad," rekindling the version the two had performed on his recent Land of Hope and Dreams tour. Grohl and Matthews returned to the stage, and Springsteen's set became an ensemble of generations of American rock musicians gathered around the protest-song tradition. Together they tore into two classics of the canon, Creedence Clearwater Revival's "Fortunate Son" and Neil Young's "Rockin' in the Free World." When the last chords faded, they stayed onstage for a final coda as the rest of the day’s performers joined them onstage. Baez and Springsteen duet during the concert Paul Morigi—Getty Images The festival was a reincarnation of the spirit of Guthrie, Seeger, and the musicians who advocated for working-class dignity and civil rights in the mid-twentieth century. Morello stepped forward to explain what they were about to sing—and why: Guthrie’s "This Land Is Your Land," but with the verses often omitted from popular renditions, in which the narrator encounters a "Private Property" sign and Americans standing hungry outside a relief office. The lines restored the song's original edge—an unofficial patriotic anthem that also challenges the country to make good on its promise that America belongs to everyone. It was the version Springsteen sang with Seeger at Barack Obama's inaugural concert in January 2009, and one he resurrected last spring at Music America: The Songs That Shape Us, his concert at Monmouth University celebrating the nation's 250th anniversary, where he recruited the kind of star-level talent the President had been unable to marshal to Washington for the occasion. For Morello, the song also evoked his mother, Mary Morello, who recently died at 102. A child of the Great Depression, she possessed an activist spirit, he said, feeding the homeless, helping recovering addicts earn their GEDs, and supporting her son's early punk bands. "Her fingerprints were all over this," Morello told me. "Her DNA was in every bit of this. I don't think it was any accident that this concert happened in a place called Mary Land." It was also there, of course, because of its proximity to the White House, and so they closed with John Lennon's "Power to the People." Morello, Springsteen, and company wanted Trump to hear them, at a precarious moment for the President and the MAGA movement, with the midterms weeks away and his poll numbers at their lowest point of his second term. But the festival's message was ultimately a call to agency, a reminder that in a democracy it is the voters who get to determine their destiny. "History is not something that happens," Morello told the crowd. "History is something we make."

The story about her daughter crashing the simulator says a lot about how she worked. Hamilton wanted a safeguard against starting a prelaunch program during flight; she was told the astronauts were too well trained to make that mistake. Then Lovell did it on Apollo 8, and her team had to help recover. Anyone who's been told "the user won't do that" should recognize that argument. She took the possibility of failure seriously, and the priority handling in her team's Apollo 11 software meant an overloaded computer could keep doing the work needed for the landing. Imagine watching that happen during the actual descent, knowing the recovery mechanisms your team built were being put to the test. The job posting was created to itemize the exact skillset this developer had, and the exact job duties this developer did. Any candidate that didn't meet the requirements *exactly* was rejected. I'm not sure I'd call it "fraud" per se. Companies usually comply with the letter of the law, just not the spirit. It's all in the definition of "qualified". If a company defines "qualified" as a person who has the exact characteristics of the H1B person they want to hire (or keep), then they are keeping the letter of the law, even if not the spirit. The law cannot mandate how wide or narrow the "qualifications" must be, leaving a nice big loophole for companies to walk through. Using a loophole does not by itself constitute fraud. We now expect graduates to have masters degrees and experience Microsoft did not require masters degrees for entry level dev jobs. It's common for Indian/Chinese folks to _have_ masters degrees, because going to the US for graduate school is a common "first step" in the process to get their feet on US soil - F1 student visas. The way it typically works for an Indian or Chinese software engineer to get into Microsoft (or any of the majors, I assume) - get 4 year degree (real or fake) back home- get accepted to US grad school; get student VISA (F1)- do grad school- look for US company that will sponsor/supply an H1-B slot- get OPT status for STEM (up to 3 yr extension of residence permit w/ industry work authorization)- start at new company under H1-B status- start the Greencard process Now, here is what is structurally fraudulent about this. 1) The F1 visa is intended for students who plan to leave when they are done. It is not intended to be the on-ramp into the US labor force.2) the OPT extension to the F1 VISA is not intended to be a long term resident work permit. They max out at 3 years for software people3) Greencard sponsorship in this scenario is supposed to be downstream of the assertion that there is no human being already in America willing/able to do the work. From a company POVThe converion rate from OPT to H1B varies; the # of H1B lottery slots are fixed, and people who run out their OPT clock without securing an H1-B lottery slot are allegedly supposed to go home. The corps know that. So the OPT period essentially turns out to be a "no risk try out" where the company gets to see who is productive and supplicant. This continues once someone has an H1B; that person knows that the moment they lose their job, they have n days to find a different H1B slot before they are subject to a removal order. An H1B friend explained it to me: as long as he's on H1B, he never says no to working late; he never says no to taking the extra incident case, he never says no. So he wants a Greencard for safety/security reasons; so he can stop being as much of an indentured servant. The colleges love this system because F1 students can be charged full price to attend. The companies love this because they get indentured servants with a tryout period. And because they have an infinite supply of young 3rd world labor who is fighting over the scraps of available slots, which causes dramatic wage suppression. When they find a gem, they can move forward with a fraudulent PERM claim and start the greencard sponsorhsip process. There may be other things going on here. The Antidote to Our Anti-Democratic Supreme Court

The Antidote to Our Anti-Democratic Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
America needs bold ideas to break the Supreme Court’s grip on democracy. Enter … the shadow court. Please enable javascript to get your Slate Plus feeds. Donald Trump brags about his boundless freedom while the same Supreme Court that handed him that power has curtailed everyone else’s. Confidence in the court has cratered to historic lows, and waiting for a justice to retire is not actually a plan. Could a “shadow court”––a body set up to critique and even to rival SCOTUS—rescue democracy? In her new book, The Shadow Court: Rescuing Democracy from the Supreme Court, Fordham law professor Julie Suk argues that the court isn’t just making some bad decisions—it’s working exactly as designed. Drawing on constitutional courts in democracies around the world that rebuilt the rule of law after falling to fascism and authoritarianism, Suk outlines a bold institutional answer: a shadow court that would issue advisory opinions on the same issues the Supreme Court decides, giving lawmakers and the public a concrete alternative to judicial supremacy. Dahlia and Julie also discuss how America’s legal and constitutional systems continue to fail women (also by design), from the stalled Equal Rights Amendment to Justice Alito’s claim that women’s reliance on Roe was merely “abstract”—and why the path to real change might run through state ratifying conventions, rather than state and federal institutions that have failed to reform themselves. There’s a Reason Pete Hegseth Chose the Fort Hood Shooter for His Grotesque Publicity Stunt
2026-10-09

There’s a Reason Pete Hegseth Chose the Fort Hood Shooter for His Grotesque Publicity Stunt

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Execution by firing squad is an especially brutal form of the death penalty. A promise to publicize the execution to the masses reeks of a bygone era of American disgrace, amplifying that brutality. On Thursday, the Pentagon announced a plan to do just that when it carries out the execution of Nidal Hasan. Hasan was a member of the military when he murdered 13 soldiers at Fort Hood in Texas. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has scheduled Hasan’s execution for Dec. 3. “Of course we’re going to do it at Fort Hood,” Hegseth crowed. “And do it on a Thursday and we’re going to do it at 1 o’clock—which is when he did it—and it’s going to be soldiers with rifles executing the firing squad.” The plan to livestream the proceedings is unprecedented in the United States. As the New York Times explains, “Releasing video of executions is most often associated with extremist militant groups like the Islamic State. …There are few countries that hold public executions.” The most prominent of those countries is Iran. Amnesty International is right to say that “executions in public serve no legitimate purpose and only increase the cruel, inhuman and degrading nature of this punishment.” That is precisely the point of the Trump administration’s plan for Hasan’s execution. Hegseth says that he wants the American public to watch because they need to understand the “serious consequences” for Hasan’s crime. But livestreaming Hasan’s execution will not be about transparency or a desire to help the American public better understand what the government does in our name. Instead, it will be the kind of spectacle of cruelty that has become a staple in the administration’s use of power, like having masked Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents roaming American streets or proudly posting videos of the American military killing people in boats off the coast of Venezuela. Public executions were once a staple of American life. From the beginning of the republic, they were carried out in town squares across the country. They were supposed to be moments of public education when citizens could witness justice being done and in which the condemned would deliver a “gallows speech,” acknowledging the sinfulness of the crime and repenting for what they had done. Parents and teachers would often bring children along to teach them a lesson. The events would sometimes feature a choir, a preacher giving an “execution sermon” and the condemned, who was typically a man being hanged, giving a long speech to the crowd. Occasionally executioners even achieved a kind of celebrity status. Arkansas’ George Maledon was so well-known that he was dubbed the “Prince of Hangmen.” Throughout Maledon’s career as a hangman, Arkansas newspapers regularly reported his identity and his role in executions. But by 1936 when Kentucky carried out the last public execution, all that changed. Their educative purpose had given way to a circus-like atmosphere with thousands of people assembling to witness a gruesome event, often tinged with the brutal white supremacy of the Jim Crow south. The New York Times recalls the event, which included an audience of 20,000 people, this way: Kentucky was the only state still conducting public hangings, a fate reserved for what was viewed by Kentucky’s citizens as the most heinous crime: rape. The condemned was a young black man named Rainey Bethea. He had been convicted of raping an elderly white woman, a wealthy widow who was found choked to death in her bedroom. Bethea’s execution carried with it the taint of a lynching as had previous public executions. And these executions seemed simply uncivilized, the sort of thing that decent people would not want to see. Once new execution technologies like the electric chair became the preferred execution method, putting people to death in the town square was no longer feasible. Scholars have claimed that there is something very different about the visceral experience of witnessing the state putting someone to death and the experience of watching that happen on television or a video feed. Wendy Lesser argues that such a viewing experience would be voyeuristic, allowing viewers to see from a safe distance something that is itself shameful. What Hegseth intends to do would indeed be shameful. It would be the equivalent of the government producing and distributing a snuff film, a film that captures a real killing and uses it to appeal to viewers’ sadistic impulses. Unlike lethal injection, the effects of which are often hidden behind a medicalized facade, the brutality of the firing squad is exactly what Hegseth wants Americans to see. Hasan will be seated in a chair with a hood placed over his head with a paper heart affixed to his chest. A group of anonymous men, each carrying identical rifles, will stand behind a wall cut with gun ports and on command shoot their high-powered rifles. The damage done to Hasan’s body will be devastating. That’s why even Vice President J.D. Vance says he won’t watch it. As though a nod to those racist public spectacles of yore, Hasan’s Muslim background almost certainly explains why Hegseth is so determined that he be the first to endure such a punishment in such a public way. In the end, though, a brutal public execution will not bring honor to the American military or to this country. It will unfortunately only confirm the widespread view that our country has lost its mind and abandoned any pretense to decency in its dealings with its citizens, or those of other nations. All of us will pay the price for Hegseth’s unspeakably vile publicity stunt. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. The Saving Grace of Trump’s Revenge Tour
2026-10-09

The Saving Grace of Trump’s Revenge Tour

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Just eight days into Donald Trump’s first term, the legal expert Benjamin Wittes coined a phrase that would come to describe many of the president’s actions: “malevolence tempered by incompetence.” Wittes’ idea was that Trump’s inattention to detail and ignorance of how government works often undermine his malign aims. More than nine years later, the phrase still applies—this time to Trump’s efforts to use the power of the federal government to punish his political enemies. A president determined to weaponize his office is, obviously, a dangerous perversion of how American presidents should behave. Yet two new investigations reveal both the malevolence and the incompetence of this president’s ongoing revenge tour. The first effort reflects one of Trump’s main preoccupations: his 2020 election defeat. After Trump claimed fraud and egged on the crowd that sacked the U.S. Capitol on Jan. 6, some of his own officials turned against him. One of them was Cassidy Hutchinson, a former aide to Trump’s then–chief of staff, who testified to a House committee investigating the attack that Trump wanted magnetometers near the White House removed to let potentially armed rioters join the crowd. On Thursday, we learned that Trump’s Justice Department is reportedly trying to convince a grand jury in D.C. to charge Hutchinson with a crime or crimes, which could include lying to Congress. It’s not yet clear what prosecutors think Hutchinson lied about, but threatening a former subordinate with jail time in an attempt to rewrite the history of your coup attempt is as nefarious as it gets. And while it’s certainly possible that the grand jury will indict Hutchinson, several of Trump’s other efforts to turn the justice system against his enemies have fallen flat. Grand juries in Washington have refused to criminally charge Democratic lawmakers who urged service members to disobey illegal orders, a protester accused of throwing a sandwich at a federal immigration agent, and several others. Still other indictments—against New York Attorney General Letitia James and former FBI Director James Comey—have fallen apart under judges’ scrutiny. The DOJ itself has also struggled to find pretexts on which to hang indictments of other Trump foes and is hemorrhaging staff as career prosecutors balk at taking part in nakedly political investigations. Unlike in his first term, Trump is surrounded today by true believers who are earnestly trying to realize his revenge fantasies. But the law keeps thwarting them, and the Hutchinson case could become the latest example. Still, the president has shown a dogged willingness to try, try again. His second effort to go after a political foe this week is actually a redo of an earlier one. On Friday, Trump announced a White House committee to investigate Lisa Cook, a Federal Reserve governor, over allegations of mortgage fraud leveled by one of the president’s own appointees. The committee, which is stocked with Trump appointees, is set to hold a hearing next month about whether Cook should lose her job. Trump, who has accused the Fed of political bias, seems to be gunning for Cook in part because she’s a Joe Biden appointee. But he’s also targeting her now because his previous attempt to fire her flopped. In a rare adverse ruling for the president, the Supreme Court temporarily blocked Trump from removing Cook in June, saying she hadn’t had sufficient opportunity to contest the claims against her (she denies them). But a majority of the justices also signaled that they don’t like the idea of presidents removing Fed officials on a whim because the central bank’s independence is key to setting sound monetary policy. So even though Trump’s new committee looks like a kangaroo court, Cook’s future is still very much in doubt. One reason the president’s revenge tour keeps flailing is that it’s largely a product of his delusions, which keep running smack into the brick wall of reality. But the possibility that Trump will fail to nail both of his latest targets doesn’t mean what he’s doing is benign. The mere fact of being investigated can exact a toll in stress and legal fees, and even watching Trump fall short could discourage others from standing up to him. We’re also less than halfway through Trump’s second term, and if his first is any indication, things could get rockier toward the end. If Democrats retake Congress next month, it may leave Trump more isolated and inclined to lash out. Buckle up! Slate was an early adopter of the listicle, that now-familiar internet format. Over the years, the magazine has ranked everything from Super Bowl halftime shows and horrible wedding songs to pre-YouTube viral hits and Declaration of Independence signatures. (For those who prefer the Winter Games, Justin Peters has you covered, with rankings of the best and worst sports—and the events ranked by the likelihood of a dog winning.) Kathy Hochul Says New York Needs to Close the “Cornell Seven” Loophole. That’s False.
2026-10-09

Kathy Hochul Says New York Needs to Close the “Cornell Seven” Loophole. That’s False.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. A civil lawsuit alleging binge drinking, rampant drugging, and gang rape at Cornell has sparked a firestorm on social media and front-page headlines. The incident occurred two years ago. Since then, the university has investigated the “Cornell Seven”—the seven frat brothers directly involved—and imposed discipline ranging from expulsion to compulsory essay writing. For many, this should never have been a campus matter but one for our criminal justice system. There has been particular ire for the reported criminal law “loophole” that the district attorney claimed tied his hands in this case, preventing initial charges. This is a red herring. Even with the current law, prosecutors had everything they needed to charge the alleged perpetrators in this case and potentially win conviction. Allow me to explain. Politicians have reacted predictably to this case with claims that criminal law is not tough enough, and prosecutors need more tools. Slamming Tompkins County District Attorney Matthew Van Houten for declining to charge the men, Gov. Kathy Hochul urged the Legislature to “close the loophole” that keeps prosecutors from convicting people who have sex with the intoxicated and the insufficiently participatory. I recall a time not too long ago when progressives and campus feminists agitated for reducing mass incarceration and the reach of America’s racially biased policing and punishment system. But such institutional concerns evaporate in the face of stories that invoke justifiable outrage against the nauseating misogyny of fraternity culture but also invite gendered ideas about women’s sexual vulnerability, ruination, and squeamishness over sexual impropriety. The impulse to avoid acknowledging that victims chose any part of a harmful sexual encounter produces overbroad criminal laws that leave little room for female sexual agency. To be clear, there are no intoxication or consent “loopholes” in New York sexual assault law. New York’s regime prohibits forcing others into sex (“rape”), imposing sex against their will (“sexual misconduct”), and having sex with “physically helpless” people (“rape”). If members of the Cornell Seven used force or threats to compel sex, or even just proceeded without Jane Doe’s consent, they committed a crime, regardless of whether she or they were drunk. Contrary to the DA’s analysis, New York law does not require unequivocal verbal refusal for sex to be nonconsensual. Perusing the DA’s justification for declining prosecution, one readily sees facts that could support charges. Jane Doe, surrounded by larger men, felt intimidated and unable to leave. She swatted hands away and said “no”; she hid under covers and became “9 out of 10” intoxicated. The DA could have brought a criminal case. We need not create a new crime of sex without an “affirmative expression of consent”—one that would render consensual, even desired, but “yes”-less sex criminal—to get at the wrongdoing alleged here. The same can be said about intoxication. Sex with a person intoxicated to the point of helplessness is already a crime—in fact, first-degree rape. A drunk but conscious person can be “physically helpless” if intoxication leaves them “unable to communicate unwillingness”—a factual determination New York courts have left to the jury. Jane Doe had been drinking heavily when she arrived at the frat house, but she was able to consider and agree to the threesome. The epistemic mismatch between her idea of what would transpire and the frat brothers’ is truly tragic. Doe had reportedly intended to hook up with Matthew Ingalls and agreed for Jonathan Newell to join in, “excited” by the prospect that they might be gay. Far from fulfilling a Heated Rivalry fantasy with male friends who “had her back,” she entered a dystopia of manosphere misogyny. Based on Doe’s legal complaint, she became the victim of a fraternity culture bent on getting one over on women as an act of toxic homosocial bonding, as evidenced by a group chat that attempted to reduce her to “free pussy.” Indeed, research finds that college men often have sex they don’t want because of pressure to conform to masculinist norms. Doe’s friends-with-benefits scenario shattered, according to the complaint, as the situation grew increasingly coercive, nonconsensual, and she approached or crossed the threshold into intoxicated helplessness. Unfortunately, the current conversation, including the call to broaden criminal law, accepts the dyad where a woman is either a complicit “slut” or a helpless, and later ruined, victim. The men’s defenders insist that Doe is the true deviant who eagerly agreed to kinky—even painfully kinky—sex, then lied about it because she was shunned from a party. A strain of “consent to some is consent to all” even runs through the DA’s explanation for his decision not to charge. But the outraged rejoinder has not generally emphasized that women can be sexual agents who agree to some activity and are assaulted in the same encounter, or that unwise and unpleasant but consensual sex is different from, yet can cross into, criminally wrongful sex. Instead, the lawsuit and many internet pundits deny that Doe had the capacity to make choices at all. She moved through the world requiring protection from drinking, from going to meet a guy she was interested in, and from agreeing to unconventional sex. So eager are online feminists to spare Doe the stigma of the girl in the threesome—a stigma we should be fighting, not reinforcing—that they insist that, despite Doe’s reasonable rationale for agreeing to it, she could not have been thinking straight. Doe herself described the binary, recalling reading an account that she was “gang raped” and thinking, “That’s the first time I had ever heard the narrative change that I wasn’t a whore and that I was assaulted.” So what exactly do Hochul and company mean by “closing the intoxication loophole”? Do they want to criminalize sex with someone who is intoxicated but appears lucid and able to agree, as Doe initially did? Given that neither a defendant’s own drunkenness nor their reasonable belief that the other consented is a defense, it behooves us to be clear about how drunk is too drunk, because that is the only line that matters. If both parties are sufficiently intoxicated when they have sex, each is a victim and a perpetrator. That both believe the sex was mutually desired is immaterial. Making intoxicated but not incapacitated sex a crime would grant prosecutors enormous authority, given how often drinking and sexual activity go together, especially among the young. Which groups will constitute the “bad guys” against whom prosecutors wield their newly minted power to prosecute drunk sex—and will they look like the Cornell Seven? Broadening criminal law to encompass widespread sexual behavior should happen only after forthright public debate. It should not be done in a viral reactive moment and couched as merely closing a loophole that doesn’t even exist. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
A House Candidate Says She Was Raped While Campaigning. It’s a Horrific Account—and an Urgent One.
2026-10-09

A House Candidate Says She Was Raped While Campaigning. It’s a Horrific Account—and an Urgent One.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The story Bernadette Greene-Placentia has told reporters and constituents about what happened to her on an early August morning is the exact kind of thing women are raised to fear: a stranger rape, with violent and terrifying men coming out of the darkness and attacking a lone woman. Greene-Placentia, though, isn’t an average woman. She’s running for Congress, and as she campaigns for Arizona’s 8th Congressional District Seat, she’s also speaking out about what happened to her. Doing so is brave. But it’s also a reminder of why representative democracies have to be representative. Women the world over live with the white noise of threatened sexual violence, a reality so pervasive it becomes nearly invisible. That threat shapes how we move through the world, who we trust, and where we go. And it shapes our politics. Greene-Placentia says that she was attacked while out repairing campaign signs around 2 a.m. Two men snuck up behind her, hit her over the head, and raped her. Her memory of the events, she says, are spotty—she believes she was concussed. It took her some time to even tell her own family, let alone the police. Eventually, though, she began talking about what happened. And now she’s gone public. Nearly every woman, I suspect, has had the kind of heart-pounding moment in which we fear being attacked in the way Greene-Placentia says she was. It’s the reason so many of us walk to our cars with our keys poking through our fingers, why we tense up when someone walks too closely behind us on a dark street. It’s the reason so many of us make our lives smaller, or at least more expensive: Why we pay for taxis instead of walking home, why we attend self-defense courses or forgo night shifts, why we leave parties early or skip solo trips, why we carefully plan running routes and hiking paths, why the unluckiest among us wind up with thousands of dollars in therapy bills and lost wages. One in five American women will either be raped in her lifetime or survive someone attempting to rape her. I think it’s safe to say that close to five in five will fear being raped. Young women—the group most at risk for being raped—are also the country’s most pro-choice voters. That isn’t to say that the prospect of sexual violence is the single deciding factor for making young women particularly supportive of abortion rights. But it stands to reason that the possibility of getting pregnant pushes many young women into the pro-choice camp. And realizing that many abortion opponents want to criminalize the procedure even in cases of rape—something so many women fear—no doubt influences many women’s politics. Greene-Placentia is running against MAGA incumbent Abe Hamadeh, who had, in an earlier run for Arizona attorney general, said he would prosecute abortion providers under Arizona law. At the time, that law was among the harshest in the nation and had no exceptions for rape or incest. Hamadeh’s comments were controversial, and he narrowly lost the election; Arizona’s abortion law has also since been liberalized. But Hamadeh is clear he opposes abortion. There are of course plenty of women who oppose abortion, too; some even head the nation’s most influential anti-abortion groups. But it also doesn’t seem a coincidence that as the Republican Party has become more male, it has also become much more anti-abortion. And while women have surged into elected office, it’s largely been as Democrats. Even women who embrace traditional conservative principles (small government, free markets, the family as the primary organizing unit of society, a muscular foreign policy) or MAGA conservative ones (whatever Trump says) cannot escape the reality of living in a female body, which is that even if you make all the “right” decisions, you can still be subjected to extreme male violence—and if that violence is rape, it can be compounded by a pregnancy you do not want to bear. Many of the women of today’s conservative movement, even those who once found hyper-masculine and cartoonishly misogynist MAGA antics to be funny and edgy, are coming to the realization that they are not the exception to the MAGA woman-hating rule—that even if you’re a cool girl, you’re still a woman surrounded by misogynists, and you’re the object of their hate. Ten years ago this week, a tape came out of then–presidential candidate Donald Trump boasting about grabbing women’s genitals, and then his administration went to great lengths to withhold the Epstein files, attacked abortion rights, and intervened on behalf of the Tate brothers, arguably the world’s the most famous and virulent misogynists, who are also criminally accused rapists and sex traffickers. (They deny the accusations.) It’s no wonder Trump’s support among women is tanking. Women are not inherently virtuous, and we certainly aren’t inherently liberal. The country should have more female legislators because the country’s legislatures should represent the country. But the country should also have more representative—and more female—legislatures because women really do have life experiences that are different than men’s, and that shapes how we understand politics, policy, and the law. Central to those differences are our ability to get pregnant, our physical vulnerability to crimes including rape, and the shadow of fear that follows us as a result. Greene-Placentia’s story is an experience that, heartbreakingly, millions of women have also survived—and that some haven’t. She describes an experience that nearly all women fear. That she’s speaking out about it puts one more crack in the lie that women who are raped should carry any shame. And it is another reason more women should be making the laws that shape women’s lives. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Microsoft leans on open weight model from Chinese AI lab to challenge Jev
2026-10-10

Microsoft leans on open weight model from Chinese AI lab to challenge Jev

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
The first version of Microsoft-Decision-1 is based on Qwen3.5-9B, but the next will sport homegrown tech, Redmond reassures Microsoft has joined the Jev fan club, an accidental group of companies that share a common desire to be recognized for their own decision models. Jev, announced by TypeSafe AI three weeks ago, is a large language model (LLM) tuned to respond to certain types of questions with a limited range of responses, rated by probability. Due to its speed, relative affordability, and response constraints, it's well-suited for a variety of business applications where open-ended text of uncertain accuracy might be undesirable. One of the selling points of Jev is that decision models don't hallucinate in the way that standard LLMs do. But decision models can make errors and their popularity has already prompted researchers to explore how those errors might be magnified. Nonetheless, they have their uses. The attention lavished on Jev prompted other companies to declare that they too have decision models to offer, even though machine learning researchers have long been able to create classifier models for probability-based decisions. OpenAI said its Decisions API has entered public beta. Cloudflare chimed in with its Clef model. Strands trotted out Strands Decider 2B, "a small, open source, decision model." Liquid AI introduced d1. Perplexity launched its Decisions API. Snowflake talked up its own decision model. Then there's Surogate Rune and H2O.ai's H2O-Lightning-4B, a decision model built on Qwen3.5-4B. All told, more than 100 such models are now vying for attention. "Decision models are quickly emerging as an important new category in AI," said Achint Srivastava, VP of software engineering in the Office of the CTO at Microsoft, in a blog post on Friday. "Unlike LLMs, which are designed to generate text or reason through complex problems, decision models are purpose-built to deliver structured outputs that software can immediately act on. And once you understand that capability – making decisions and classifying things at very low cost with high performance – all kinds of useful tasks get unlocked." Microsoft's entrant into the race is called Microsoft-Decision-1, which is offered via Microsoft Foundry and, soon, via OpenRouter. Redmond's decision model, like H2O's, is based on a Qwen model, Qwen3.5-9B in this instance. The Qwen model family is developed by Alibaba Cloud, the cloud computing arm of Chinese tech giant Alibaba. Microsoft, for reasons not disclosed, said it will soon rebase the Decision-1 on its models and those from OpenAI. The US software giant claims its model is 2.5x faster than H2O-Lightning-4B and 2.8x faster than Jev in its latency test, leads the pack in accuracy (83.5 percent) on 36 benchmarks, ranks second (behind Quyet-1.0-Large) in confidence score (92.2 percent), and is more than 20x cheaper than OpenAI's GPT-6 Sol in text classification tasks. Input tokens cost $0.042 per million tokens and output tokens are free. "Now that agentic AI is a reality, we’ve seen that cost plays a major role in how people decide to use AI," said Srivastava. "And it’s increasingly important to choose the right model for the right job." ® Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Low-band frequencies can reach devices inside buildings, while 2 GHz orbiters could extend coverage beyond cell towers SPONSORED FEATURE: While linking the GPUs in a datacenter is a challenge, coordinating thousands of them to work efficiently over hundreds of kilometers presents a new set of difficulties. AWS AgentCore security undone by prompt requesting credentials
2026-10-09

AWS AgentCore security undone by prompt requesting credentials

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier Bob, possibly the same Bob whose conversations with Alice draw so much interest from eavesdropping Eve, was browsing a site we'll call TechHub. The site hosts an AI agent served by Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Bob asked the agent for help understanding the content of a URL, a credential endpoint – in raw JSON, if you don't mind. The endpoint returned data from the Instance Metadata Service (IMDS), which provides metadata about cloud instances and VMs at providers like AWS, Azure, and Google Cloud Platform. The metadata includes details like region and availability zone, subnets, system images, security groups, public keys injected during spawning, but also potentially more sensitive details like user data and security tokens. IMDSv2 addresses some of these risks, but back when Bob was browsing late last year, Bedrock AgentCore still used IMDSv1. The metadata provided to Bob by the helpful agent contained the agent's temporary credentials. So Bob loaded them onto his local machine, and remotely enumerated the company's other agents in that AWS region. He then logged into the Amazon Elastic Container Registry (ECR), pulled the agent container images, and ran each as root to inspect the source code. The stolen credentials also allowed Bob to discover the memory resources available in that AWS region, including the ones used by agents. From these, he's able to extract the users and their agent sessions – their conversations. Researchers at Zenity Labs disclosed their findings to AWS in December 2025. "We discovered that agents deployed through AgentCore could access their instance's IMDS endpoints," said Tamir Ishay Sharbat and Lana Salameh in a blog post. "This meant that an external attacker with nothing more than chat access to a single exposed agent could send a single prompt, extract its IMDS credentials, and use them to take over all AgentCore agents in the same AWS account and region." The basic problem, they explain, is that the Firecracker MicroVM used by AgentCore failed to provide sufficient network isolation. So an attacker – I know you're thinking it was Mallory, but it was really Bob – could direct the agent to carry out a server-side request forgery (SSRF) attack by fetching temporary AWS credentials for the IAM role assigned to the workload. And because the default AgentCore role was overpermissioned – it was scoped to all AgentCore resources in the region rather than a single agent – anyone in possession of the temporary IAM credentials could launch other agents, read sessions, write agent memories, and fetch secrets from AWS Secrets Manager. "By leveraging the IMDS credentials we could send direct API requests to create new memories across different agents and users," said Sharbat and Salameh. "These in turn would persistently alter agent behaviour and hijack the agents’ goals across future sessions." The Zenity researchers told Bob's tale, or something like it, to AWS last December, then followed up in January 2026 with details about AgentCore being overprivileged. On April 12, 2026, AWS responded to the security biz that its report was "informative" and closed the report, noting that as of February 14, 2026, AgentCore had been updated to use IMDSv2 exclusively. AgentCore's excessive permissions remained in place at least until June 22, 2026, when Zenity checked in and found the issue hadn't been remediated. A final review by Zenity occurred on September 29, 2026, at which point the biz observed that AWS had addressed the extant problems, clearing the way for Bob's hypothetical adventure to finally be told. Following publication, Amazon wrote in to say that Zenity’s research misrepresents documented behavior as a vulnerability, suggesting that developer error would be required to enable the attack. We’ve asked for clarification since that’s not the scenario Zenity has described. ® Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Low-band frequencies can reach devices inside buildings, while 2 GHz orbiters could extend coverage beyond cell towers SPONSORED FEATURE: While linking the GPUs in a datacenter is a challenge, coordinating thousands of them to work efficiently over hundreds of kilometers presents a new set of difficulties. Microsoft 365 subscribers set to lose up to 4 TB of OneDrive storage
2026-10-09

Microsoft 365 subscribers set to lose up to 4 TB of OneDrive storage

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again Microsoft 365 subscribers on Family, Premium, or Pro tiers who make full use of their OneDrive storage allotments consider this a warning: It's fixin' to shrink. Redmond published a support page this week detailing changes to Microsoft 365 shared storage allotments, and while the company is painting them as offering greater flexibility for users, they also represent a massive reduction in capacity. The former storage allotment schema offered 1 TB per user on all three of those tiers, with a cap of six users, for a total of 6 TB. The new storage quotas will be shared among all users on those accounts, still with a six-user cap. Family or Premium plans now get 2 TB of storage space to share among up to six users. Pro tier users will have to share 5 TB. In effect, that means fully shared Family and Premium subscriptions will lose up to 4 TB of combined storage capacity, while Pro subscriptions will lose up to 1 TB. Some existing customers may qualify for temporary bonus storage. On the plus side, there's no longer a fixed 1 TB cap per person, provided the shared pool has room. "The goal is to provide a more flexible experience," Microsoft offers as its justification for the move. "Shared storage helps ensure that one person doesn't run out of space while other members have unused storage available." That might be great for heavy users on an account, who can now go over a 1 TB cap, but if, say, four people on a family plan are all using 500GB and someone wants to upload something else, that's too bad. You can always buy more OneDrive space, of course. Microsoft appears to understand that users might see this as a reduction in storage, posing an FAQ question: "Is this change a reduction in storage?" The answer again highlights "flexibility," and helpfully notes, "For example, if the subscription owner doesn't share the plan, they can use the full shared storage allotment." (Individual plans already exist but top out at 1 TB.) Microsoft also appears to understand that the move might cause a backlash, and has included subscription cancellation steps right on the support page for the change. New Family, Premium, and Pro tier subscribers as of Thursday will see this new storage allotment schema. Existing subscribers will be hit with the change when their plans renew on or after May 2, 2027. Microsoft said it will remind users of the change as their renewal date approaches. Customers are not thrilled about this move. A thread on Reddit discussing the change has nothing positive to say about it, with commenters noting that they're likely to cancel their subscriptions rather than let them renew and face a storage reduction and a need to clean up files. Much of the Microsoft365 subreddit, as of writing, is filled with posts complaining about the move, with one of the top posts reminding people to turn off their automatic renewal to avoid getting hit with the storage slash. The Windows maker has a semi-regular habit of slashing cloud storage allotments for its 365 customers. Office 365 used to come with unlimited cloud storage, but that offer ended after some users actually took unlimited to mean unlimited. Storage tiers have been further reduced over the years, most recently in 2024 when McGill University drastically slashed students' OneDrive allowances to 20 GB following Microsoft's changes to education storage allocations. In other words, don't get too comfortable with 2 TB of shared storage for six people – that deal, too, may be altered further. ® Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Low-band frequencies can reach devices inside buildings, while 2 GHz orbiters could extend coverage beyond cell towers SPONSORED FEATURE: While linking the GPUs in a datacenter is a challenge, coordinating thousands of them to work efficiently over hundreds of kilometers presents a new set of difficulties. SpaceX to buy key spectrum that could help Starlink Mobile become major US cell carrier
2026-10-09

SpaceX to buy key spectrum that could help Starlink Mobile become major US cell carrier

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Low-band frequencies can reach devices inside buildings, while 2 GHz orbiters could extend coverage beyond cell towers SpaceX is looking to take on the incumbent mobile networks in the US with a combination of low-band spectrum that can penetrate indoors together with the 2 GHz spectrum supported by its Starlink Gen2 satellites for bandwidth. The rocket-flinging operation says it cut a deal to acquire a nationwide low-band spectrum license portfolio of up to 14 megahertz of paired spectrum in the 800 MHz band, which will pave the way for Starlink Mobile to become a major cellular carrier in the US. SpaceX agreed to acquire the spectrum licences from telecoms investment firm Grain Management, which bought them from T-Mobile. The transaction is subject to FCC approval and other customary closing conditions. In its FCC filing [PDF] regarding the transaction, SpaceX says it intends to roll out its own terrestrial infrastructure for this purpose, operating standard wireless equipment such as antennas on towers, rooftops, and other structures to provide reliable signal coverage. Financial terms of the agreement were not disclosed. We asked SpaceX for further details and will update if we get answers. Also this week, America’s telecoms regulator, the Federal Communications Commission (FCC), approved Starlink’s application [PDF], filed last year, to put into orbit an additional 15,000 satellites that are optimized for 2 GHz spectrum globally. The operator says this will allow it to bring high-speed service directly to unmodified devices anywhere in the world. It builds on SpaceX’s $17 billion purchase of spectrum from satellite biz EchoStar last year, which netted it 50 MHz of exclusive S-band spectrum in the US, as well as the firm's global Mobile Satellite Service (MSS) licenses. Taken together, these two moves could pave the way for Starlink Mobile to build a hybrid satellite-terrestrial network, with the low-band spectrum helping to reach subscribers inside buildings, while the 2 GHz spectrum used by its satellite constellation could extend high-speed coverage to areas beyond the reach of cell towers. It is understood that most existing mobile devices already support the 800 MHz band frequencies, which SpaceX describes as currently underused, meaning subscribers can connect without having to upgrade. “Starlink Mobile can now become the first network operator to deploy both satellite and terrestrial spectrum. One network that provides Americans with a reliable service indoors, outdoors, in cellular dead zones, and everywhere in between,” the firm stated in an update. According to finance news site MarketWatch, shares of the incumbent wireless operators fell following the news. It says that Verizon shares were down almost 6 percent, while AT&T and T-Mobile US shares were down more than 6 percent. T-Mobile kicked off a Direct-to-Cell service for US customers using Starlink’s existing satellite constellation last year, offering it even to customers of rival networks for $15 per month. A report out earlier this year found that the number of direct-to-device connections rose nearly 25 percent between July 2025 and March 2026, but a separate report cast doubt on how widespread adoption of the technology will be. Juniper Research pointed out that it is inherently niche, kicking in only when the terrestrial signal fails, and it does nothing to solve poor indoor coverage – something that SpaceX’s 800 MHz move aims to address. ® Tokens transmitted in metadata, weak VM isolation, and expansive permissions make hacking a lot easier What Microsoft giveth, Microsoft taketh away, again and again PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Low-band frequencies can reach devices inside buildings, while 2 GHz orbiters could extend coverage beyond cell towers SPONSORED FEATURE: While linking the GPUs in a datacenter is a challenge, coordinating thousands of them to work efficiently over hundreds of kilometers presents a new set of difficulties. Growing pains: how distributed AI training changes the network between datacenters
2026-10-09

Growing pains: how distributed AI training changes the network between datacenters

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED FEATURE: While linking the GPUs in a datacenter is a challenge, coordinating thousands of them to work efficiently over hundreds of kilometers presents a new set of difficulties. Large-scale AI training has already escaped the confines of a single datacenter. Google said Gemini was trained synchronously across clusters in multiple locations; Microsoft has connected AI data centres in Wisconsin and Georgia into what it describes as one distributed AI supercomputer; AWS has connected AI compute clusters across wide areas to allow Anthropic to build Claude models; Meta has built high-capacity datacenter interconnects to support model training; and CoreWeave and Google Cloud recently announced cross-cloud training with a private interconnect, with Azure likely to follow later in the year. This growing geographic spread of model training is partly due to the limits of single datacenters or campus clusters, which can be strained as they try to meet the compute and power demands of new models. Cisco estimates that training models today can require clusters with tens of thousands of GPUs. By 2030, the largest individual frontier training runs could draw 4-16GW of power, according to researchers at Epoch AI. Distributing compute allows hyperscalers and datacenter operators to build in locations with more available power or space and fewer planning constraints. At the core of an LLM is a neural network with billions of numerical parameters, or weights, that are adjusted during training. A batch of training data passes through the model, which makes a prediction. The system measures the error and calculates how the weights should change. The weights are updated and the process starts again. At the scale of modern models, this work is distributed across thousands of GPUs and other accelerators, such as AWS Trainium and Google TPUs. Different GPUs can process separate batches of data or different parts of the model. But they cannot work entirely independently: at various points they must exchange results and synchronize before the next stage of training can begin. What networks carry between the accelerators is generally not the text or images used to train the model, but large arrays of numerical data such as gradients and intermediate results. Thousands of accelerators may need to exchange and combine that data at roughly the same time. "If you look at any training job, it is basically a repetition of compute and synchronization," explains Ramesh Sivakolundu of Cisco's Silicon One architecture team. "You compute the weights, communicate those weights to synchronize every GPU in the cluster, and then restart the computation. That means bursty traffic happens periodically throughout the training process." Thousands of accelerators can finish a computation phase and start communicating at the same time. This can lead to "incast" problems where many senders converge on the same destination and traffic arrives faster than the next link can handle. The fundamental issue lies with synchronization. In a synchronous training job, one group of GPUs cannot simply ignore a slower group and continue independently. The network-wide exchange must complete before the participating GPUs can move on to the next stage. A network delay therefore becomes compute delay. "You don't want the network to be the bottleneck in the training process," says Sivakolundu. "The idea is to keep the GPUs fully occupied and not have the network introduce additional latency into the overall training program." That does not mean that every instance of network congestion or packet loss brings the entire model training to a complete halt. However, a delayed or dropped flow may require retransmission, prolong a collective communication operation, and leave other accelerators waiting. More serious failures can require training to return to a saved checkpoint. "Inside a single datacenter, you can assume you have built a full network connecting the GPU racks with the bandwidth you planned for," says Itamar Gold, director of product management at Cisco. Gold’s point is that with scale-across, coordinated AI workloads may have to traverse a more constrained inter-site fabric than the network within each facility. Instead, he says, traffic destined for the other facility may be funneled through what is effectively a narrower pipe. A synchronized burst can turn that pipe into a temporary choke point. Traditional DCI connects otherwise independent datacenter environments, and is typically built for redundancy, reach and workload distribution. The traffic that runs over them is often replication traffic or application data, and the flows are asynchronous, meaning one flow does not have to finish in lockstep with the others. In scale-across AI training, the inter-site network becomes part of a coordinated distributed computation rather than simply moving data between otherwise independent systems. It is therefore essential to have sufficient bandwidth and predictable delivery. Within a rack, bandwidth requirements can be enormous – up to 800Gbps-1.6Tbps - but the distances are short. High-speed serial lanes combine to make a GPU cluster appear as a single compute unit. When the AI fabric is extended across an entire facility, “scale out”, racks will still require connections up to-1.6T. At these data rates, copper is generally confined to short reaches. Connections across rack rows and datacenter floors instead use pluggable optics, with scale-out links extending from hundreds of meters to around 2km. “Scale-across” extends a coordinated AI fabric across multiple facilities, making inter-site network behavior part of workload performance. Depending on the architecture, those links may span metro or regional distances, and potentially farther depending on the architecture. According to Cisco modelling of large distributed AI deployments, aggregate bandwidth requirements can reach roughly 14x a conventional DCI baseline. For starters, short-range datacenter optics are not designed to carry 400-800 Gbps signals over hundreds of kilometers. For these distances, operators need coherent optics that use sophisticated modulation and high-performance digital signal processing to keep high-rate optical signals usable over metro and regional fiber spans, compensating for physical impairments that become more pronounced with distance. The number of ports matters too. Cisco estimates that connecting two 100MW AI sites could require 12,000 to 32,000 coherent optical ports at the scale-across layer, compared with roughly 1,000 to 2,000 for conventional DCI between comparable facilities. At 400G or 800G per port, that adds up to multi-petabit aggregate capacity. Moving that much data is also a power and space problem. Coherent pluggables put the coherent transponder function directly into router or switch ports, avoiding a separate bank of standalone DWDM transponders. Cisco says this reduces the additional rack space, power and cooling needed for the inter-site optical layer which is an important consideration when the AI factories themselves are already power-constrained. Even with sufficient bandwidth and suitable optics, distance adds latency. If congestion develops, the network needs time to signal the sender to slow down. Over a local AI fabric, feedback can arrive quickly. However, if the connection is stretched across 100 km of fiber, considerably more data can be transmitted before the sender even learns that there is a problem. Cisco calculates that on an 800 Gbps connection spanning 100 km, around 100 MB of data can already be in transit during that feedback interval. Cisco argues that these longer feedback loops increase the value of networking silicon designed with substantially deeper buffering. The shallow-buffer switching architectures designed for very low latency inside a datacenter have less capacity to absorb traffic while congestion feedback crosses a long-distance link. "The reason you need deeper buffers as the distances get longer is that you don't get feedback about the transfer of packets for a longer period of time," says Sivakolundu. "If you don't buffer deeply enough, you can end up dropping and retransmitting packets, increasing latency." Deep buffering absorbs a temporary burst or disruption while traffic management and congestion signaling address the underlying problem. Sustained congestion still needs more capacity or a change in how traffic is distributed. The same buffer also helps with oversubscription. If the combined capacity feeding into an inter-site connection is greater than the capacity of the link itself, a synchronized burst can arrive faster than the link can drain it. Buffering gives that excess traffic somewhere to wait without getting dropped while congestion controls respond. Rather than dividing the available buffer into fixed amounts for each port, a shared buffer is a common pool of packet memory that can be allocated wherever congestion appears. That lets a busy link absorb a larger burst without dropping packets. "The longer you're reaching out, the more you may need to buffer," says Gold. "You also need the flexibility to put as much buffer as possible where it is needed, perhaps on one port or a few ports where you've identified a problematic flow. Our approach is a single, fully shared buffer." AI training does offer one useful characteristic: much of its collective communication is repetitive and therefore more predictable than DCI traffic. Cisco's approach is to use proactive congestion management to steer or schedule traffic before a predictable burst creates a problem, while retaining deep buffering as a safety net for transient congestion and unpredictable events such as a link failure. The two approaches are complementary: one tries to avoid the queue while the other gives the network somewhere to put the traffic when a queue forms. Cisco’s scale-across architecture uses the Silicon One P200, a 51.2 Tbps programmable, deep-buffer routing processor included in the Cisco 8223 and Cisco N9000 platforms. Those systems can be paired with Cisco's 400G and 800G coherent pluggable optics for the long-distance links, while open line systems such as Cisco Open Transport 3000 and Cisco NCS 1014 handle the optical transport layer where required. Coherent pluggables generate the DWDM wavelengths directly from the routing system, while the line system amplifies and carries those wavelengths across the fiber plant. For links requiring multiple parallel fiber pairs, Cisco's Open Transport 3000 uses a multi-rail design that combines the optical components for multiple rails onto one line card. Cisco claims this reduces power per rail by 75 percent and rack space by 80 percent. Where a separate transport system is required, the NCS 1014 can provide 12.8 Tbps of capacity from a 1RU line card. The P200 has a programmable run-to-completion network processor and P4 tooling, which Cisco says allows protocol support, telemetry and other packet-processing features to be developed in software rather than making every change dependent on a new silicon generation. For scale-across, where operators are still testing different topologies and traffic-management methods, "the same programmable flexibility can let network functions evolve without every new requirement forcing a chip replacement,” says Sivakolundu. Cisco also includes hardware-based protection for inter-site traffic in that design, its argument being that encryption should not create another processing bottleneck for the training fabric. A key part of Cisco's pitch is the co-design. A networking ASIC is defined years before the finished router reaches a datacenter. Cisco argues that because its silicon, system and optics teams sit within the same company, requirements for port density, buffering, power, telemetry and the optical interfaces can feed back into the chip design rather than the system team receiving a commercial ASIC and working out what it can build around it. That does not mean scale-across has settled on a single approved architecture. Cisco itself divides the problem into campus, metro, and regional deployments, and different operators are experimenting with various combinations of networking and training techniques. Implementations vary significantly by operator, including distance, topology and workload design, while the common problem is maintaining coordinated AI performance across sites. The technology is still at an early stage, however. "We are seeing early deployments now, but it is a process and I think it will take a few years," says Gold. Designing a network for distributed training will not eliminate the latency of distance, but it can keep that interconnect from becoming the bottleneck. That gives hyperscalers and datacenter operators the flexibility to add compute where power and space are available, while still treating multiple sites as part of the same training infrastructure.
