India calls Vance’s ‘indentured servants’ remark deeply offensive
2026-10-09

India calls Vance’s ‘indentured servants’ remark deeply offensive

The Next Web | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
The US Department of Labor’s Frances Perkins Building in Washington, DC India’s foreign ministry has called US Vice President JD Vance’s description of foreign workers hired by Microsoft as “indentured servants” deeply offensive. Spokesperson Randhir Jaiswal read out the ministry’s statement at its briefing in New Delhi on Friday. “Resorting to terminology that carries painful historical and colonial legacy connotations is deeply offensive,” Jaiswal said. On Thursday, Vance announced that the Labor Department was suspending eight companies from the PERM programme. PERM is usually the first step when an employer sponsors a foreign worker for a green card. The eight are Microsoft, Adobe, Cognizant, Infosys, Tata Consultancy Services, Wipro, HCL and France’s Capgemini. Vance said Microsoft had replaced laid-off American workers with lower-paid foreign ones. Microsoft said most of its H-1B filings last year were for people already working there. Jaiswal said such descriptions were unwarranted. They ignore that Indian professionals in the US are highly educated and skilled contributors to its economy, he said. The ministry said PERM is distinct from the H-1B visa programme. The suspension does not by itself affect the validity of H-1B visas, it said, or the status of visa holders and their families. It could affect the green card process for eligible employees of the eight companies. “The steps announced by the US do not advance the shared ambitions of both countries,” Jaiswal said. He said India continues to follow developments. The ministry added that talent mobility helps both economies, including US companies and their shareholders. Labor Secretary Keith Sonderling said the department will not accept new PERM applications involving the eight or process pending ones. Vance said the suspension would last “as long as it needs to”. Microsoft is the biggest user of PERM, according to a GeekWire analysis of Labor Department data. The department ruled on 3,161 of its applications in the fiscal year to September 2025, more than for any other employer. It ruled on 39 from Amazon and three from Google. Across all employers, the department certified nearly 140,000 PERM applications in fiscal 2025, Axios reported. About 30% were for software developers, computer systems analysts and data scientists. The workers most exposed are H-1B holders near the visa’s six-year limit, immigration lawyers told Business Insider. Progress towards a green card is what lets many of them extend their stay. “Most people will either be able to wait it out or have time to switch jobs,” said Loren Locke, a lawyer in Atlanta. Federal law lets the Labor Department suspend an employer on its own for only 180 days, Ted Chiappari of Duane Morris told Business Insider. After an investigation, it can bar an employer for up to three years. Kripa Upadhyay of the Seattle firm Buchalter said she expected lawsuits against the PERM suspension to start by next week. The suspension follows a proposed $103,265 fee on new H-1B visas. In August, OpenAI agreed to a $3.2m US settlement over how it advertised PERM jobs. Microsoft shares fell about 1% on Thursday and Adobe’s rose nearly 4%, Axios reported. In Mumbai on Friday, Indian IT stocks rose. By late morning, TCS was up 5.5%, Wipro 3.2%, HCL 2.9% and Infosys 2.7%, IIFL reported. TCS had reported its quarterly results the day before. India’s IT industry body Nasscom said Indian tech companies in the US had significantly reduced their use of H-1B visas, Reuters reported. It said relatively few of their workers seek green cards through PERM. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Trump gives Musk, Huang, Su and Brin the National Medal of Science

Trump gives Musk, Huang, Su and Brin the National Medal of Science

The Next Web | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
President Donald Trump at the launch of America.gov at the Andrew W. Mellon Auditorium in Washington, D.C., 29 September 2026 Six technology leaders received the country’s highest honours for science and technology from President Donald Trump on Thursday, the White House said. Elon Musk, Sergey Brin, Jensen Huang and Lisa Su got the National Medal of Science. Michael Dell and Satya Nadella got the National Medal of Technology and Innovation. Trump presented the medals at the Science: A New Golden Age Summit in Washington. Michael Kratsios, director of the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy, joined him on stage. Musk’s citation honours his engineering work in space exploration, intelligent systems and communications, and in American manufacturing. Brin’s citation names his foundational algorithms for information retrieval and data processing. Huang’s citation credits him with turning the graphics processing unit into a general-purpose computing architecture. Su’s names her work on processor architecture and chiplet-based integration, which powered the world’s first exascale supercomputer. Dell’s citation credits him with advancing personal and enterprise computing and building a worldwide technology company. Nadella’s citation names Microsoft’s renewal for the cloud era and the hyperscale infrastructure behind American super intelligence. Super intelligence is the administration’s term for AI since a September executive order. “Innovators like these six awardees are the architects of the future,” Kratsios said. Presidents have given the National Medal of Science since 1963, and the technology medal since 1985. Past winners of the science medal have mainly been academics and research scientists, the Associated Press reported. Trump called the group “national treasures”, AP reported. He compared them to his uncle, John Trump, an MIT professor who received the National Medal of Science in 1983. “We live in an age of American wonders and technological marvels, and with the help of the brilliant men and women here today, we will always be on top,” Trump said, according to NBC News. He called Musk “our modern-day Thomas Edison”, The Hill reported. Each of the six has given money in some form to Trump or to Republican campaigns and causes, CNBC reported. Five of the six were born outside the US, AP reported. Musk was born in South Africa, Brin in Russia, Huang and Su in Taiwan, and Nadella in India. Dell was born in Texas. “I was an immigrant, and my parents brought us here to pursue the American dream,” Huang told NBC News. Hours before the ceremony, Vice President JD Vance announced that the Labor Department was suspending Microsoft from the PERM programme. PERM is the first step in sponsoring foreign workers for green cards. AP reported that Vance was asked about the timing. “We’re going to continue to support Microsoft and have a great relationship with Microsoft,” Vance said. Nadella and Dell both thanked Trump on X. Dell wrote that he started his company 42 years ago with $1,000 in his dorm room. “I’m humbled to stand alongside so many giants of American innovation,” Nadella wrote. At the same summit, AI companies pledged $2.4bn in credits and AI tools for federal science, including Nvidia’s $1bn commitment. “These recipients are helping ensure America keeps leading the world in innovation,” White House spokesperson Liz Huston said. micro1 commits $1bn to buy company data for training AI agents
2026-10-09

micro1 commits $1bn to buy company data for training AI agents

The Next Web | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
AI training company micro1 will spend $1bn over the next 12 months buying and licensing operational data from companies. The San Francisco company announced the plan on Friday. Citi and Hercules Capital are providing the capital. The money goes through micro1’s Company Data Partnerships programme. The company uses de-identified company data to build reinforcement learning environments that reflect real business operations. In them, AI models and agents learn to work through workflows, make decisions and complete complex tasks. “Real business operations involve incomplete information, competing priorities and exceptions that require judgment,” micro1 said in its announcement. The company said the $1bn will widen the range of industries and workflows its environments cover. It said the initiative gives businesses a new revenue stream from the operational knowledge they have built over years. The programme’s page lists the data micro1 wants. That includes standard operating procedures, knowledge bases, internal documents, CRM data, project histories and quality assurance processes. It also wants records of how teams make decisions, and human feedback on AI outputs. Payment depends on the quality, uniqueness and value of the data, the company said. The page lists three payment levels. A qualified partnership earns more than $100,000. Large datasets, or ongoing work across several teams, earn more than $500,000. Highly unique, proprietary data earns more than $1m. Companies keep ownership of their underlying data. The company said it removes personal information “when relevant”. It may also rewrite some datasets synthetically to loosen their link to the original records. Companies can review samples before micro1 uses the data. The programme targets companies with more than 30 employees and established documentation. The company gives priority to US companies, then other Western markets, and the material must be in English. The company calls itself a data lab that helps AI research companies and enterprises train frontier models and evaluate agents. It runs three in-house labs, one of them for robotics. In September, micro1 made a competing bid for Spirit Airlines’ customer records. The court-appointed privacy official in Spirit’s bankruptcy has since backed Google’s $10m deal for that data. Elon Musk’s SpaceX has also weighed buying data from startups to train its AI models. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Anthropic signs CrowdStrike, Hitachi and 9 more to defend power and water
2026-10-09

Anthropic signs CrowdStrike, Hitachi and 9 more to defend power and water

The Next Web | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Anthropic will give its frontier Claude models, engineers and threat research to the companies that guard critical infrastructure. That means power grids, water utilities and transport networks. The Critical Infrastructure Defense Program is the first part of the Anthropic Cyber Mission. The company announced it on Thursday as a long-term effort to support defenders. The second part is OSS Scanner, a free service that scans open-source code with Anthropic’s strongest models. It sends the findings straight to maintainers, without a human checking them first. Accenture, Booz Allen, CrowdStrike, Deloitte and Dragos joined as founding partners. So did Hitachi, Insane Cyber, Nozomi Networks, Palo Alto Networks, PwC and Rockwell Automation. Anthropic said they are the consultancies, security companies and equipment makers that operators already rely on. Several are already using Claude to fix vulnerabilities, it said. The programme targets operational technology: the controllers, control software and industrial networks that run plants for decades. Operators often cannot take these systems offline to patch them, so known flaws can stay open for years. Anthropic will start with a small group of providers to learn what works. “Critical infrastructure is hard to defend in many ways that AI cannot fix,” Anthropic said in its announcement. Hitachi said in its own statement that it will join the programme’s working groups. It will also share its operational technology expertise. The Japanese group signed a strategic partnership with Anthropic in May and joined Project Glasswing in June. Anthropic did not say who will pay for the computing, or whether partners get free model access, Axios reported. Axios had the announcement first. It added that it is unclear how partners will test fixes without disrupting utilities. In June, Anthropic started a similar programme for US state, local, tribal and territorial governments. It said it has since given Claude models and technical support to more than half of US states. OpenAI put $1bn behind cyber defence for water utilities and community banks in September. OSS Scanner is opt-in and modelled on Google’s OSS-Fuzz. Enrolled projects get periodic scans from Anthropic’s most capable models, including Claude Mythos, according to a Frontier Red Team post. Each report carries a proof of concept, an explanation and, where possible, a suggested patch. Anthropic expects more than 90% of the findings to be real. Its penetration testers checked 97 critical and high-severity findings from 48 projects. Of those, 85 met the bar for its disclosure process. Another 11 were real but repeated known issues, and only one was a false positive. Over six months, Anthropic’s models flagged more than 29,000 candidate vulnerabilities. Its staff could only review about 6,000. Nearly 5,000 unchecked reports went to maintainers who asked to receive everything. Todd Ouska of wolfSSL said his project got 74 reports and all but two were valid. Five became CVEs. “OSS Scanner has helped us find multiple issues in curl worthy of addressing, including one of the worst curl vulnerabilities reported in the last few years,” said Daniel Stenberg of curl. Anthropic picks projects case by case, and only takes established ones with a critical impact on infrastructure and user security. Its service agreement warns that reports may misjudge severity or propose patches that break things. Maintainers must review every report, and Anthropic caps its liability at $1,000. Anthropic’s consumer terms also apply. “Our forecast is that in two years, AI will favor defense,” Anthropic said. The company added that this may not hold in the near term. Exploiting flaws has become cheaper, while verifying and fixing them still depends on people. In Glasswing, months often passed between finding a flaw and fixing it, and on running machinery a fix can in rare cases wait decades. Last week Anthropic said a Chinese model nearly matches Mythos at cyber exploits. This week it merged Glasswing into its expanded Cyber Verification Program. The Defender Advantage Fund it launched in August keeps OSS Scanner free. Anthropic has also funded the Python Software Foundation, the Apache Software Foundation, and Alpha-Omega and OpenSSF through the Linux Foundation. CrowdStrike, one of the new partners, already works with both labs. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Vesta raises $30M for AI agents that work on mortgage decisions
2026-10-09

Vesta raises $30M for AI agents that work on mortgage decisions

The Next Web | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Vesta has raised a $30M Series B to expand AI agents that handle mortgage origination work, including underwriting decisions, with three customer lenders among the investors. The same software sold in Europe would be high-risk under the AI Act, and GDPR already limits automated decisions of this kind. Vesta has raised a $30M Series B to put AI agents inside mortgage lenders. Conversion Capital led the round, and three lenders already using the software invested alongside Citi Ventures and Andreessen Horowitz. Chief executive Mike Yu said demand had “exploded in the last year” and revenue is up twelvefold, he told TechCrunch. Vesta has raised $85M in total and puts its market share below 5%. Lenders on its platform originate more than $100B of mortgages a year, HousingWire reported. About 40% of tasks running through Vesta are now handled by agents and automated workflows. Those tasks reach further than paperwork. Lenders use the agents for document review and data validation, and some for underwriting decisions, condition clearing and closing package review. The appeal is cost. A US mortgage takes around 40 days to close and roughly $11,000 to produce, mostly labour. The Mortgage Bankers Association has put origination costs above $10,000 for years. Yu credits one model. He named Anthropic’s Claude Sonnet 4.5 as the breakthrough, saying it followed configured instructions over the long stretches that mortgage work demands. Automated lending decisions carry a record. The Markup found in 2021 that US lenders were 80% more likely to deny Black applicants than comparable white ones, after controlling for 17 financial factors. Yu says responsibility does not move. Lenders remain accountable for underwriting whatever software they use, he said, and every action and the reasoning behind it is logged for audit. Europe does not leave that to the vendor. The AI Act puts systems that evaluate the creditworthiness of natural persons in its high-risk class, and mortgage approval sits squarely inside it. That brings obligations rather than guidance. Providers face conformity assessment and registration, while deployers must assign human oversight to competent staff, keep six months of logs and run a fundamental rights impact assessment. Those duties were pushed back. Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 deferred them from August this year to 2 December 2027, enforced by an AI Office that can fine 3% of turnover. One rule is not waiting. GDPR Article 22 already restricts decisions made solely by automated means where they carry legal or similarly significant effects, and a refused mortgage is the textbook case. Europe already has a version of this. Taktile in Berlin raised $110M in June for agents that underwrite business loans, which point 5(b) does not reach because it covers natural persons. Wayve and Stellantis tune one AI driver for Fiat, Jeep and Maserati
2026-10-09

Wayve and Stellantis tune one AI driver for Fiat, Jeep and Maserati

The Next Web | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Wayve chief executive Alex Kendall at Wave by Vento in Turin, 9 October 2026 Every car will one day need an AI driving system, just as it needs seatbelts and emergency braking, Wayve chief executive Alex Kendall said. He spoke with Stellantis chief executive Antonio Filosa at Wave by Vento in Turin on Friday. Morgan Stanley’s Adam Jonas moderated. “I think over time we’re going to see this kind of technology be required in vehicles like a seatbelt, like an emergency braking system,” Kendall said. The technology is affordable enough for mass-market cars, and what happens on roads today is unacceptable, he said. “So it’s really morally wrong to not have some system, some intelligence in the vehicle to save those lives,” Kendall said. Stellantis and Wayve announced their partnership in May. Stellantis is putting the Wayve AI Driver into its STLA AutoDrive platform for hands-free, supervised driving. The first launch is targeted for 2028 in North America, Stellantis said. At Wave, the companies gave demonstration rides in a Fiat 500e and a Maserati Grecale. The idea to put the system in the Fiat 500 came from a handshake about five months ago, Kendall said. Wayve tunes the AI to drive “Italian style”. He said he drove the Maserati on a track in Turin the night before, in pouring rain. Wayve wants systems that run on less than $1,000 of hardware so they can reach mass scale, he said. Wayve also has a deal to put its AI into Mercedes-Benz production cars. It runs a supervised robotaxi service in London with Uber. Each Stellantis brand will drive differently, Filosa said. AI will make each vehicle a self-learning machine. A Jeep will learn differently from a Peugeot or a Maserati. Customers will feel the difference between a Maserati and a Jeep, Kendall said, though both are built for safety first. The partnership works because the two sides complement each other, Filosa said. Wayve brings the AI. Stellantis brings brand, vehicle and safety knowledge, and the scale that brings costs down. Stellantis develops a product in 24 months today, and AI can shorten that, he said. Kendall sees supervised driving as a stepping stone to eyes-off and driverless cars, not a separate business. Starting supervised introduces customers to the technology and builds the data needed to improve it, he said. It also gives a head start on supply chains and hardware, which take years in the car industry. To go unsupervised, a system must be safer than a safety-conscious human driver, he said. That is a higher bar than an average driver. Wayve has training data from over 100 countries and has tested in 600 to 700 cities. It proves safety in simulation first, with its generative world model, Gaia, before it deploys. Performance is growing by more than an order of magnitude a year, he said. The scaling curves look like those of language models. Kendall wants Wayve to be the intelligence layer for vehicles, and later for robotics too. Last month, he said Wayve’s AI would reach Mercedes cars within two years. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.
